- **5 Aug (🛡️ THE CRITICAL BATCH — #617 #620 #621 #622, and the read-first pass rewrote two of them):** four launch-critical items in one founder-authorised PR (*"i need it in one prompt as i am heading out"*), driven by **two founder rulings that reshaped every deadline**: ① **no real-money walkthrough** — *"i am not running the real money walkthrough as i dont have that money"*; mitigation recorded: open the checkout to the card screen and cancel (proves keys, price and page), the webhook side is covered by tests, and **client #1's first payment watched live IS the money walk**. ② **Claude access ends 18 Aug** while send-day is ~25 Aug — so every 🤖/🤝 deadline moved to 18 Aug and A14 became *runbook by 18 Aug, executed founder-solo*. **#617 PECR:** UK sole traders are individual subscribers, so cold-emailing one is a breach; pure classifier, fails **SAFE** (the opposite of #618, deliberately), word-boundary matching because *"Salted Foods"* passing on `ltd` would hand a sole trader an exemption they do not have. **⚠️ It was wired at two enrol paths and there were three** — `autoEnrollLead` was found while wiring #620 and it is **the path a client's own approval takes**, i.e. the one that runs on send-day; without it the client is charged for a lead we can never legally email. **#620:** every refusal was already NAMED and **nothing rendered it**, so *"every draft refused"* and *"nothing happened"* were the same picture; now persisted to `operator_audit_log` (no migration) and rendered in words. **#621:** the send-day runbook, every step read out of the live code, `CHECK:` for anything unverifiable. **#622 — the read-first pass changed the answer:** D1 said *"boxes 3+4 into the engine"* and **there is no two-box limit in the code at all** — the engine already uses every sendable box; boxes 3+4 are held out solely by their `warming` status, and that guard is correct. So D1 is a **status flip on the day, not a build**, and A6's ladder was already enforced at exactly 50/day — only the literals needed naming. **Corrected the same session:** the stale C3 row (said "awaiting preview" a day after shipping) and the line-64 *$99 Stripe description* claim, void on the same catalogue screenshot that killed A7.
- **5 Aug (🧾 #619 — THE VIDA BOARD WAS TELLING THE FOUNDER TWO THINGS THAT WERE NOT TRUE, AND HE CAUGHT BOTH ON A SCREENSHOT):** the live Clients board ticked **"Paid $299 ✓"** on the house account — which has never paid us anything — and flew a red **SUSPEND** badge on an account that #618 had just made exempt. Both are the same shape: a surface reading a value that means one thing and printing a sentence that means another. **①** `PAID_TX_TYPES` includes `manual_grant` **on purpose** (it is what ENTITLES a comped account), so the board read entitlement and printed a payment — the #613 failure again, one screen over. Pure `fundedVia()` now separates them, `real` requires a purchase type **AND** a provider reference, and ambiguity resolves **downward** to `comp`: understating what came in is correctable, claiming money that never arrived is what we are ending. **②** #618 fixed the cron and stopped, leaving **three display copies** calling `coldState` raw — and its own test file had written the warning down (*"two copies of who is exempt"*). The answer was not a fourth copy: **`coldView()` is now the one place the clock and the exemption meet**, so the next surface inherits it instead of re-deriving it. **⚠️ AND IT TURNED UP A THIRD MONEY LIE NOBODY WAS LOOKING FOR:** the *"$ in"* figure was `99 + … * 4` **hand-typed**, so since the 3-Aug move to $299 the operator's board had been understating every paying client by **$200** — the exact failure #563 records, sitting in a line that had not been read since. Interpolated now. **The gate was deliberately left alone:** `hasFunded` still counts a comp, because flipping it would send Client Zero back to *"Waiting on their $299"* — us chasing ourselves. Display honesty, not a gate change. Fails open; a real client on the identical 59-day clock is still suspended and a real payment still reads Paid — both asserted, because a one-direction test would pass on deleted code. 28 tests, red-proved 6 ways. **Docs:** A7 is **VOID, and the founder killed it with a screenshot** — his Product catalogue shows 9 active products, 0 archived, every price matching the nine env vars, and **nothing at $99**. My archive-or-edit instruction was a hedge against a product that does not exist; the "$99 description" was a 3-Aug claim carried forward unverified, and *this is the exact class of thing the working method exists to stop*. Recorded: when the founder said *"we never did it and never had to do it"*, he was right and the runlist was wrong. **What he actually needs — bundle top-ups — was verified working the same session:** $299 first purchase enforced server-side, then $40/$100/$200 top-up buttons on the billing page, validated against `TOPUP_PRESETS`, wallet credited ledger-first by the webhook, first-purchase-does-not-credit-wallet proven by `pack-wallet-split.test.ts`. No dashboard SKU in the flow, by design.
- **5 Aug (🧊 #618 — THE COLD-CLIENT RULE STOPS POINTING AT US, and the reasoning that made it safe was mine and it was wrong):** the rule exists because *"a client's sender costs ~$40/month… a bill we pay to keep an inbox warm for nobody"* — a cost rule about a CLIENT going quiet while we carry their mailbox. `cold-check` skipped **only** `is_demo`, and `house-client.ts` **deliberately un-demos** Client Zero so it counts in revenue and the CSV import accepts it. Both right; together they put **our own account inside a rule written for other people's accounts. We are the nobody, and we chose our own bill.** **⚠️ #611 filed this as *"a trap for the abnormal order"*** — approvals precede a campaign, any approval resets the clock, low risk. **The founder's A4 ruling killed that argument in one sentence:** the 159 approved leads are real prospects and stay, and they are **already approved AND already enrolled**, so send-day needs no new approval — a campaign goes active ~25 Aug with the last approval **~59 days** old and the next 08:40 run pauses it, after three weeks of warming. **A piece of my own reasoning expired because a ruling changed the facts under it, and the lesson is to re-check the argument when the inputs move, not just the conclusion.** Built as a pure `coldCheckExempt()` the cron asks — **one place decides, not two**, because two copies of *who is exempt* is how the cron and the audit panel start disagreeing. House resolved **once, before the loop**, via `decideHouseClient`, **never by company name** (#584/#582). **Fails OPEN on purpose:** unresolved house → nobody exempt → the cron behaves exactly as today; failing closed would silently exempt everyone and break a rule that saves real money. Demo exemption unchanged, and **a real client on the identical clock is still suspended — asserted by test**, because the feature's whole value is that it still fires for the people it was written for. Red-proved 3 ways; 14 tests; gate green, 1,864.
- **5 Aug (🗂️ A4 RULED — THE 159 STAY, AND THAT TURNS THE COLD-CRON LANDMINE FROM THEORETICAL INTO DATED):** the founder ruled the 159 approved leads on Client Zero are *"potential clients"*, not test residue — so they are kept and no clearing build is needed. **The ruling has a consequence worth stating plainly, because it inverts the earlier reasoning.** #611's audit called the cold-client collision *a trap for the abnormal order*, on the grounds that approvals normally precede a campaign and any approval resets the clock. **That reasoning does not hold here:** these leads are **already approved and already enrolled**, so send-day needs no new approval — a campaign goes active on ~25 Aug with the last approval **~59 days** old, and the **08:40 UTC cold-check pauses it the next morning** and fires a churn-risk alert about our own account. The clock reads 39 days today and only counts up. **So A5 stops being a tidy-up and becomes a dated, pre-25-Aug item.** Recommendation recorded: exempt the house account in `cold-check` (the cron already skips `is_demo`, and `house-client.ts` deliberately un-demos Client Zero so it counts in revenue figures — the two right decisions that together created this), with a fresh approval on send-day as free belt-and-braces. The reasoning is the rule's own header: *"a client's sender costs ~$40/month… a bill we pay to keep an inbox warm for nobody"* — **we are the nobody, and we chose our own bill.** Also cleaned up: five merged local branches deleted after a stop-hook reported phantom unpushed commits (a local branch reset onto main while its remote still pointed at the merged commits — nothing was ever unpushed, verified by a zero diff against main).
- **5 Aug (🔗 THE BOOKING LINK LEAVES EMAIL 1 — a contradiction the product had been carrying since #612, ruled and removed):** #612 Part B found that this repo held **two live, opposite instructions**: the quality gate forbade a link in a first touch, and `generateSequence`'s own system prompt **ordered the booking link into step 1**. Enforcing the gate would have skipped every lead the product wrote for, so Part B exempted the client's own URL and reported it as a warn — with a *ruling owed* note, because papering over a contradiction is not resolving it. **The founder ruled Option A: the gate is right and the prompt is wrong** — *"the first email's job is to earn a reply, not a booking."* The reasoning he was given, and the part worth keeping: a link to a stranger costs deliverability, and *"book 15 minutes"* is a large ask from someone who has never heard of you, while *"worth a look?"* is a small one — **the link converts far better in the REPLY, once they have raised their hand.** Both prompt sites changed; **step 3 keeps its link** because the ruling was about the first touch only; and the new instruction **carries its own reason**, since an LLM told *no link* without a reason is one rewrite away from helpfully adding one back. The exemption, the warn and the now-unused `bookingUrl` lint option are all removed — **an option nothing reads is an invitation to re-introduce the exemption by passing it again.** **The tests that pinned the old ruling were rewritten to pin the new one rather than silently flipped**, with the reason in each, and the fixture's link moved to step 2 so it stays a genuinely good example of what the product writes. **⚠️ And the honest limit, found by tracing rather than assuming:** a lead whose draft is refused twice is skipped with the rule named in `skip_reasons` — but **a grep of the portal and admin apps returns ZERO hits**, and both enrol endpoints are machine-facing, so **no screen renders it**. On send-day a systematic refusal would read as *"no enrolments happened"* instead of *"every draft was refused for this rule"*. Recorded as LAUNCH-PAD **C8** rather than claimed as handled. 75 tests, red-proved 4 ways; gate green, 1,850 tests.
- **4→5 Aug (🧾 #615 — THE DEFAULT STATE OF EVERY CLIENT WAS "UNDOCUMENTED", AND THAT IS THE STATE THAT COSTS 20%):** founder-ruled — *"as part of onboarding we capture their company information… all clients."* **Why it is a tax question rather than a form question:** we are a UK Ltd, and for a BUSINESS customer the place of supply is where the customer belongs — an overseas business is outside UK VAT, an EU business self-accounts under the reverse charge. **But you must hold proof they are a business.** No proof, consumer treatment, 20% on top. And today `company_registration` and `vat_number` sit as **optional fields on a Settings page nobody is required to visit**, so undocumented is the default for everyone. **The sole-trader problem is the part worth recording:** a legitimate client may genuinely have no VAT number, and forcing a value makes them **invent one** — an invented tax ID is **worse than a blank**, because it looks like evidence so nobody chases it, and it is the thing an auditor reads. So "not registered" is an explicit recorded answer: **blank means nobody asked; the sentinel means we asked and this is the answer.** Ticking the box AND giving a number is refused; typing the sentinel by hand is refused. A sentinel rather than a boolean column because **the schema is frozen** — same shape as `PERIOD_END_SENTINEL`. **The constraint that keeps it from becoming an outage, and it is enforced by test: it gates ONBOARDING COMPLETION AND NOTHING ELSE.** No approval, payment or send may consult it — every client who signed up before this existed has empty fields, and locking them out of the product they pay for over a form would be a far worse bug than the one it fixes. **Enforcement beyond onboarding is left as a founder decision and deliberately not taken in code.** **⚠️ AND A FINDING HE MUST RULE ON: `staging` is 46 commits behind `main`.** Branching the preview off it would have deployed a **46-commit-stale product** to the preview site — so this went to its own branch off current main as a PR held for his walk, rather than silently merging 46 commits to the site clients see. Client-facing, so 🟡 until he previews it, never straight to 🟩. Red-proved 3 ways (the empty step refused · the tick storing a real answer rather than falling through to blank · nothing in the money path gating on it — that third proof was **invalid on the first attempt**, because I injected the reference inside a comment and the checker strips comments before asserting, which is correct behaviour by the test and a bad proof by me).
- **5 Aug (🧾 THE MORNING AUDIT — five merged items wore 🟡, PR #1269 grew conflicts, and the runlist moved ONTO the launchpad):** the founder caught two real failures in one look. **① Docs were behind:** #611, #612, #613, #614 and #616 had ALL merged to main and every one still wore 🟡 — the flip-on-merge ritual was told to the founder and then not tracked; five flips run 5 Aug (🟡→🩷 via script). **② PR #1269 conflicted with the overnight batch** — 4 files (`shared/index.ts`, inventory, KIND-MASTER, LAUNCH-PAD), and it was **predictable at planning time**: two overnight branches minting inventory rows at the same anchor and both appending a `shared/index.ts` export. The batch merging first made the second PR unmergeable. My planning error, resolved by union-merge (both exports, both rows, both log lines) — recorded so the next overnight batch puts ALL doc edits on ONE branch. **③ The standings table now LIVES ON THE LAUNCH-PAD** (founder: *"i work off launchpad — this table needs to be on the launchpad"*): the old patchwork plan table replaced by THE RUNLIST — A path-to-25-Aug (14 rows) · B the UK-company nervous system (6) · C build states with the two PARTIALS stated as partial (seat-cap has an API and **no screen**; seat delete is a ruling) · D blocked (4, including the staging-46-behind ruling). Also corrected from the morning report: my claim that *"the owner can set the seat cap in Command Centre"* was **overstated — only the API exists**; and C7 (PECR/GDPR pass) is named as the ONLY pre-25-Aug build item not started.
- **4→5 Aug (🌙 THE OVERNIGHT BATCH — #613 · #614 · #616: the console did not tell the truth about money, and the seat limit was a rule nobody could see or change):** the founder's *"full audit of all expenses"* sweep found three things the product had never modelled, and all three were the same shape — **a number presented with confidence that nothing had ever checked.** **#613 — EVERY MONEY ROW RECORDED THE PRICE WE QUOTED.** `routes/stripe.ts` wrote `amount` from the checkout metadata and the subscription path from a hardcoded constant; **nothing in the repo had ever read `balance_transaction`.** So a discount, a refund, a dispute or a currency conversion was invisible and **no figure in the console reconciled to the bank** — on a $299 sold in USD into a GBP account, roughly **£210–£213 actually arrives**. Settlement facts are now stamped on each ledger row and a read-only panel puts the two numbers side by side. **⚠️ It is annotation, not columns, and that is recorded as the lesser fix it is:** the honest schema is three new columns, the schema is frozen, so the facts go in the row's existing `note` — readable and greppable, **not summable**. **It also cannot make old rows honest**; it begins with payments from now. **#614 — THE MARGIN WAS COMPUTED FROM LITERALS TYPED INTO A PAGE.** `estStack = 690` and `estCostPerClient = 95`, plus four hardcoded strings on the Revenue page — one of them the **$138 infra line the founder personally caught on 3 Aug as overstated by $87/mo**. Meanwhile `docs/CASHFLOW-LAB.html`, the model he actually maintains and corrects against invoices, **was ignored by the console entirely**. The floor now lives in `@kind/shared` (the admin app cannot import from `apps/api` — the identical structural cause of #563) and **a drift test parses the doc** so the two can never diverge silently. Five **UK-company lines** exist for the first time — ICO fee, Companies House, accountant, accounting software, insurance — and **every one is tagged `unverified-secondary`, enforced by test**, because gov.uk, ICO, Companies House, Stripe, Xero and Wise were **all unreachable** (network-policy 403) when they were researched. **That tag is the $138 lesson made structural: a confident label on an unread number is worse than no number, because it stops everyone afterwards from re-checking.** **#616 — THE SEAT LIMIT WAS ENFORCED, INVISIBLE AND UNCHANGEABLE.** `companies.seat_cap` had a real guard before every rep invite, a DB default of 25, and **no write path in the entire repo** — 8 references, not one a write — and the portal type did not even declare it, so nothing could render it. The 409 at rep 26 said *"raise the cap"*, **naming a control that did not exist**. Now owner-only (a manager runs reps; what the company *pays for* is the sysadmin's call), bounded, and it **refuses a limit below the seats in use** rather than silently accepting a number that would remove nobody and only break the next invite. The dead *"Add member"* button — which navigated to a settings anchor that does not exist — now points at the real invite flow. **AND THREE OF MY OWN TEST ASSERTIONS WERE WRONG BEFORE THEY WERE RIGHT, every one caught by breaking the code rather than by reading the test:** an ordering proof that compared against the first ledger insert *anywhere in the file* (so moving a stamp above its own insert still passed) · an assertion anchored on a comment that `stripCommentsForEnvScan` had already removed, leaving it searching an empty string · and a fixed-width window that swept the legitimate write into the refusal check. **That is six such self-inflicted guard bugs in two days**, and the pattern is now unmistakable: **an assertion must be bounded by the thing whose behaviour it asserts.** Gate 6/6 green after every part; 1,826 tests. **A finding the founder must rule on: `staging` is 46 commits behind `main`**, so the client-facing VAT work (#615) could not be previewed there without deploying a 46-commit-stale product — it is a separate PR held for his walk rather than a silent 46-commit merge to the preview site overnight.
- **4 Aug (🚪 #612 PART B — THE GATE COVERED EVERY PATH EXCEPT THE ONE THAT SENDS, AND THE FIX ALMOST KILLED ALL OUTREACH):** Fable's verify of the still-OPEN PR #1267 found two gaps, **neither of which anything went red for** — a green gate said nothing about the enrol path. **① The words that actually send live on the ENROLLMENT, not the template.** `routes/figsy.ts` enrols with `appliedSequence ? buildDraftFromSequence(...) : await generateSequence(...)`, and **with no applied sequence the AI writes fresh copy per lead that nothing looked at.** Part A gated templates and the drafts an operator *reads*; the copy the send loop actually emails went straight through. Now gated at **both** enrol sites and **before `chargeFigsyEnroll`** — #332's *charge-first* means the charge gates the ENROL; it was never a licence to take a credit for a lead we are about to refuse. One regenerate, then **skip with the rule NAMED** in `skip_reasons`, because a bare *"3 skipped"* reads as a data problem and sends the reader hunting in the wrong place. **Demo exempt on purpose** — #453 leaves `next_send_at` null so a demo can never reach a human, and #329 already settled that breaking the demo removes the sales tool; exempting a path that cannot send costs nothing. **② The gate judged the WRONG ROW.** `sequenceGateFor` linted the client's newest saved sequence while a campaign carries an **applied** one (`settings.sequence`) — save a clean draft over an older bad applied one and the gate green-lit copy that was never going to send while the copy that WAS going to send went unread. **⚠️ AND THE THING THAT WOULD HAVE KILLED ALL OUTREACH, caught by reading `generateSequence` BEFORE wiring rather than after:** enrol-time copy is **rendered, not templated** — it carries the lead's real name (no `{{tokens}}` survive) and **our own system prompt orders the booking link into step 1** (*"include this exact booking link on its own line"*). Judged by the template rules, **every draft the product writes hard-fails on `no_personalisation` AND `link_in_step_1`: 100% of leads skipped, and reported as a copy problem.** Part B would have silently switched outreach off. The linter gained a **`rendered` mode** changing exactly three things — personalisation proved by the person's own details in the opening, the client's **own** booking URL not counted as a generic link **but surfaced as a WARN** (never silently forgiven), and nothing else. **🧍 RULING OWED, and it is a real product conflict rather than a lint detail: #612's rule says no link in the first touch, and `generateSequence` is instructed to put one there — one of the two is wrong, and exempting it keeps outreach working today without settling which.** **A third of my own guards was wrong, and again only red-proving found it:** the charge-ordering test split the source and compared indexes in slices that ran to the end of the file, so moving the charge ABOVE the gate found the *other* loop's charge and **stayed green** — an ordering assertion has to be bounded by the thing whose order it is asserting. Re-scoped per loop body, then red-proved. **That is three of my own guards wrong in two sessions** (the definition-matching wiring count, the one-spelling opt-out, and now this), every one caught by deliberately breaking the code rather than by reading the test — which is the argument for red proofs over careful review, stated as plainly as it can be. 68 tests, red-proved four ways; gate 6/6 green, 1,760 tests.
- **4 Aug (✉️ #612 — THE COPY GATE, BUILT BEFORE THE BOXES, AND TWO OF MY OWN GUARDS WERE WRONG):** founder-ruled *"our sequence and outreach must be world class. shit emails out = zero meetings booked. for all clients"* — and the timing is the whole point: **four Google boxes finish warming ~25 Aug, and the FIRST sends out of them set those domains' reputation permanently.** A bad first batch does not merely book zero meetings, it burns three weeks of warming and the domains with it. Every other guard in this repo protects money or data; **this one protects the only asset we cannot re-buy.** **The evidence says copy is the multiplier, not volume:** the vendor-claim research run the same evening (Gong/30MPC, 85M+ cold emails) puts the average rep at **~344 emails per booked meeting while the top quartile books 4.3× more from the same send volume** — so whether our funnel lands nearer 500:1 or 1,000:1 is decided in the words. *(That research also found not one of Apollo, Alta or Clay publishes enough to compute contacts-per-closed-win: Apollo's 50,000 is SENDS by one rep, Alta's 159 is **"influenced"** not closed-won, and both Clay cases withhold the denominator entirely. **Founder ruled the model stays at 1,000:1** — the maths is not changing off vendor marketing.)* **`lib/sequence-quality.ts` is PURE**, which is not a style preference: it is the only way to test a linter whose failure mode is *"a real prospect received this."* **Two severities, and the split is the design — HARD blocks activation** (no opt-out anywhere · spam vocabulary · a link or attachment in step 1 · no personalisation in step 1's opening · a bare "just bumping" follow-up · >7 steps · two steps the same day) **and WARN never blocks** (subject length, 50–125 words, one ask per email, Flesch–Kincaid >8, tired openers). **A warn that blocked would teach operators to route around the gate, and a gate people route around protects nothing.** **⚠️ THE WIRING IS THE PART THAT COULD HAVE BEEN FAKE:** a campaign goes live from **three** routes, and a gate wired into two of them is not a gate but a detour sign — all three call one helper, **save is deliberately NOT blocked** (work in progress must be savable; what must not happen is putting it in front of a stranger), **pausing is never gated** (stopping bad copy stays one click away), and **FIGSY's own draft goes through the same gate before it is returned**, because a draft arriving "from the AI" carries an authority a hand-typed one does not — precisely when a bad email gets waved past. The preview lints the **raw template, never the token-filled render**: linting `rendered` would report every correct sequence as un-personalised, and a rule that cries wolf on correct copy is a rule the operator learns to ignore. Fails **open** on a read error, because refusing to start a campaign over a database hiccup makes an outage look like a copy problem (#565's shape again). **AND TWO OF MY OWN GUARDS WERE WRONG — red-proving is the only reason they are right.** ① The wiring test counted `sequenceGateFor(`, **which also matches the function's own definition**, so `>= 3` was satisfied by 1 definition + 2 calls: **I deleted a gate from a live route and the test stayed GREEN.** ② The opt-out rule accepted *"won't bother you again"* and **rejected *"will not bother you again"*** — a guard that recognises one spelling of a promise teaches people to write the other. **It also broke a neighbour's test honestly:** `vida-honesty.test.ts` sliced a fixed **1,400 characters** from `/campaign/start` and went red when the gate pushed `writeOperatorAudit` past that window — **the property held; the WINDOW moved.** Rewritten to slice to the next route registration and red-proved by flipping the audit action, because the alternative (widening a magic number) is how a test eventually gets deleted. **A process burn recorded rather than hidden:** `git checkout` used twice to undo a red-proof edit **also reverted uncommitted work in the same file** — caught by grepping the file before moving on, and the fix is to checkpoint the code before red-proving, not to remember harder. 50 tests, red-proved four ways; gate 6/6 green both ends.
- **4 Aug (🛠️ #611 PHASE B — THREE THINGS ON THE BOARD WERE LYING, AND THE PRODUCT LEARNED TO DELETE):** the founder ruled on the audit table, so Phase A's *"no button acts on a finding"* rule was retired in one place and one place only. **The three panel fixes are all the same defect: a screen answering a slightly different question from the one it appears to answer.** ① The audit panel **ran itself on page load** — the founder opened Engine, four screens of findings appeared, and he said *"i didnt run anything"*. He hadn't. An audit that self-runs makes *"what does it say"* and *"it has been run"* the same event; the button is now the only trigger. ② The **`can send` chip was `has_smtp ? 'can send' : 'no SMTP details'`** — status-blind. A **warming** box with credentials saved rendered a **green "can send"** while `pickSendingInbox` refuses it outright, because *sending on a warming mailbox is what un-warms it*. **That is the exact state of all four of the founder's boxes right now**: the board and the sender said opposite things and nothing was red. It now asks the send path itself (`boxSendVerdict` delegates to `pickSendingInbox` — one rulebook, never two). ③ The **warm-up fraction was hardcoded `/14`** and the route clamped the day to 14, while these Google boxes warm **21** days — so around **18 Aug the board would have read "14/14 · ready" a full week before the row's own ready date**, on the one screen used to decide when sending starts. It now DERIVES the length from `warmup_ready_at − warmup_started_at`, and shows **no fraction at all** when there is no ready date rather than guessing a denominator. **THE WALLET, AND WHY NO LEDGER ROW.** `$3,999,038` against a ledger summing to **−$305** — inherited test grants, and not cosmetic: once the pack is used, approvals spend the wallet, so real approvals would draw on invented money and land in revenue figures. Zeroing corrects the **balance** and writes **nothing** to `credit_transactions` — no money ever moved, and a fabricated −$3.99M transaction would put a lie in the one table that IS the audit trail. The record is the `operator_audit_log` line. Then **$4,000** on as a `manual_grant`, the mechanism Client Zero is already comped through, as a **constant not a request field** — an endpoint that takes an amount is a *give any client any money* surface. **AND THE FIRST DELETE OF A NON-DEMO CLIENT ROW IN THE PRODUCT.** `purgeDemoClient` refuses anything not `is_demo` and its own comment calls that *"the whole safety of this function"*; Stripe Test and ACME are not demo rows, so that guard could not be reused and the new one had to be at least as strong. **Four guards plus a structural fifth:** `seed-wipe.ts`'s `classify` **imported, never re-implemented** (real money outranks every label — *"the flag is a human's opinion and a Stripe-referenced ledger row is a fact"*) · an explicit **id** refusal for the house account, red-proved by fooling the classifier into calling Client Zero eligible and watching it refuse anyway · an id refusal for every `is_demo` row · the client's own **company name, typed** · and **the house account must RESOLVE before anything is deleted** — if we cannot say which row is Client Zero, we are not in a position to delete another one. `wipeMbf` is imported for the table list rather than re-listed, so the delete cannot fall behind the day a table is added. **Red-proved four ways** — the confirm-phrase gate, the house refusal fired AT the house id, the chip on a warming box, the denominator on day 14 of 21 — and the three source assertions were proved by restoring each pre-fix line and watching them fail. **And the verify pass earned its keep the same evening:** the $4,000 grant button had **no "already granted" guard** — every later press was another $4,000, on the account we had just built an endpoint to remove invented money from; `disabled={busy}` only blocks a double-click, not a second deliberate press. Fixed on the same PR: the route asks the LEDGER (`priorHouseGrant`, matching the grant's own note tag; the $100 comp grant deliberately does not match) and refuses 409 with the original grant date, and the balance now moves through the **atomic `increment_wallet` RPC** — the mechanism every other money path already uses — instead of read-then-write. Red-proved by stubbing the guard call and watching the wiring assertion fail. *(A process burn recorded honestly: `git checkout` used to restore the red-proof edit also reverted the uncommitted guard itself — caught by grepping the file before moving on, re-applied, re-proved. Restore with `git checkout` only what you have committed.)*
- **4 Aug (🔌 THE INSTRUMENT SHIPPED UNUSABLE, AND A GREEN GATE IS WHY):** Fable's verify of #1265 found `HouseAudit.tsx` — the read-only audit panel built hours earlier — fetching `${NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL}/operator/house-audit` **directly**. Every operator route rejects a request without `x-admin-key`, and the **only** thing that injects it is the admin proxy (`app/api/proxy/[...path]/route.ts`), which also stamps `x-operator-email` from the verified session. So the panel was a **guaranteed 401 on every load** — an audit built to be run, that could not be run. It was the **only** component in the admin app calling directly; its sibling `HouseClient.tsx`, one file over, does it correctly. **The lesson is about the gate, not the URL:** the component compiled, the admin app built, 1,623 tests passed, and the fault was invisible in source — it only appears in a browser nobody had opened yet. **A green gate proved the code was well-formed and said nothing about whether it worked.** Fixed to the proxy, and guarded so the class cannot recur: `admin-proxy-only.test.ts` sweeps every admin component and fails any `/operator/` fetch not routed through `/api/proxy/`, with the reason attached (401 **and** an audit-trail hole). **Red-proved twice** — once against a verbatim fixture of the pre-fix line, once by restoring the **actual pre-fix file** from git and watching the sweep catch it. The checker de-duplicates by operator route rather than URL form, because an interpolated template matches two patterns and a guard that reports one fault twice teaches the reader to doubt the count. It also survives `HouseAudit.tsx` now **documenting the broken URL in a comment** — a guard that fails on its own documentation teaches people to delete the documentation.
- **4 Aug (🔎 THE AUDIT FOUND A DATED LANDMINE, AND THE ROTATION FOUND A MISSING COLUMN — #611 + #610, one combined PR by founder authorisation):** ran Prompt 29's audit fully before building Prompt 30, both on one branch because they touch the same docs and a stranded PR is how #1240 died. **#611 — the house audit is an INSTRUMENT, not a query, and that is forced:** there is no SQL access, so nobody could open a console and look at what Client Zero actually contains. Built as read-only `GET /operator/house-audit` + a Vida panel that traces every headline number to its rows and returns REAL/DEBRIS/REVIEW verdicts for the founder to rule on. **Deliberately no action button** — audit and cleanup one click apart is how the wrong row gets deleted at 11pm. **The finding is the cold-client collision.** `cold-check` runs 08:40 UTC daily and skips **only** `is_demo === true`; `house-client.ts` **deliberately un-demos** Client Zero because *"`is_demo` true would exclude it from every revenue figure AND make the CSV import refuse it."* Both decisions are individually correct. Together they leave the house account inside a rule whose own header says it exists because *"a client's sender costs ~$40/month… a bill we pay to keep an inbox warm for **nobody**"* — **we are the nobody**; there is no client to lose and no bill we did not choose. Impact today is **nil** (it pauses `WHERE status='active'`, there are none, and it returns silently having paused zero) — **impact from ~25 Aug is real**: a campaign going active with the approval clock past 30 days gets paused next morning plus a churn alert about ourselves. Approving resets the clock, so approve-then-run is safe; it is a trap for the abnormal order, on the one morning nobody wants surprises. **Founder ruling owed.** **#610 — rotation built, and it is PARTIAL for a reason worth stating:** `sendablePool` + `nextFromRotation` spread a batch least-used-first across every sendable box, each `daily_cap` respected, all-capped returning **null so the caller STOPS rather than overflowing a cap onto the very reputation the cap protects**. Warming boxes are still refused — rotation spreads across boxes that may already send, it never widens that set. **But `figsy_sent_emails` has no column naming the mailbox that sent** (it has no `client_id` either — the counters join through `leads`), so *"sent today by this box"* is unanswerable from the database and answering it is a migration the frozen schema forbids. So the tallies are **per-batch, in memory**: exact within a run, and across two runs in one day a box could exceed its own cap — bounded still by the global `coldCapReached()`. Vida shows each box's **cap** and deliberately **not** a today-count: **a number we cannot compute is worse displayed than absent.** **THE BUG I ALMOST SHIPPED, caught by writing the test before trusting the code:** rotation with no tally increment — every count stays 0, `nextFromRotation` returns the same box forever, and the feature looks implemented while changing nothing. Red-proved by deleting the line (2 assertions fire), and pinned alongside the sharper half: the tally increments **only after a confirmed send**, so a broken mailbox cannot eat the batch's quota. **The `pooled` redefinition is recorded in four places** — the Vida form's own help text, `sending-inbox.ts`, TECH-STACK and #610 — because the single thing that must not happen is a future reader seeing `pooled` and assuming a vendor rental.
- **4 Aug (🗺️ THE PLAN TO SEND-DAY LOCKED — and selling came OFF the board at the founder's order):** the evening ended with the whole path to 25 Aug in one prioritised table on the LAUNCH-PAD, owners and timelines attached, and three explicit founder rulings recorded. **① Selling is his:** *"leave the 20 messages out of this list all together. i know how to sell. i will use my own prices"* — outreach-by-founder is deliberately absent from the board, not forgotten. **② Two boxes now:** `hello@kindoutreach.com` takes the pooled slot as a documented redefinition and the send loop learns rotation (**Prompt 30**), shipping dormant until the ladder. **③ Volume beyond 2 — no workaround (standing recommendation, unruled = default):** per-box caps climb 30→50/day as sending history earns it, reaching the modelled ~100/day on two honest boxes; boxes 3+4 plug in the day migrations return, and defeating the schema for them was explicitly declined. **Also settled in plain words after the founder said, twice, that the ending had confused him:** the house account is **K.I.N.D as its own first client** — where OUR prospecting runs through OUR product from 25 Aug — and it sends **nothing** today: three locks (warming refused by the picker · `AUTO_OUTREACH_ENABLED` unset · the ladder) and a counter reading 0-sent-ever. Everything on its screen that looks alive ($3.99M wallet, 159 approved, "14 Sending") is **adopted test debris**, which is exactly what **Prompt 29** audits — read-only, founder rules line by line before any cleanup writes. The pre-25-Aug play stated once: **clean the vessel and load it during warmup** (ICP → source on the paid Apollo credits → founder approves → sequences drafted) so the first warm day sends from a ready queue instead of wasting warm reputation on list-building. **LAUNCH-PAD rewritten to this plan** — the "cannot send, no mailbox exists" headline it carried since July is retired: mailboxes exist, they are warming, and the board now counts down instead of listing absences.
- **4 Aug (🧱 THE 4-BOX PLAN COLLIDED WITH THE SCHEMA, AND THE COLLISION IS THE FINDING — #610):** entering the SECOND of four mailboxes into Vida, the founder hit `client_inboxes_one_live_per_kind`. Reading the migration rather than working around it found **two independent limits, both real and both invisible until walked**. ① `client_inboxes` allows `kind IN ('pooled','branded')` only, with a unique index on `(client_id, kind) WHERE status IN ('assigned','warming','active')` — **max two live boxes per client** — and the table's own comment explains why: it models the **CLIENT lifecycle**, a rented pooled box sending from day 1 while the client's branded box warms, switching ~day 29 with no gap. ② `pickSendingInbox` **ranks the live boxes and takes the top one**: there is **no rotation across mailboxes**, so even two would not both be used. **The four-box plan assumed a rotation the product does not have, and nobody noticed until a form refused a row.** **Consequence today is small and worth stating plainly:** Client Zero sends from `jacques@kindoutreach.com` at a 30/day cap ≈ 600/month — far above the founder's own prospecting need — and the other three keep warming in Instantly as domain-diverse spares that cost nothing extra. It bites at 100–200/day, or when one client needs more than one sender. **⚠️ It cannot be fixed by migration: the schema is frozen**, so this is recorded as a known limit rather than a task. **THE TEMPTATION REFUSED, and it is the reason this entry exists:** the obvious workaround was to label the second Google box `pooled` and slip past the index. `pooled` means a **rented, recycled vendor box that gets released back to a pool** — a pool that does not exist here — and `pickSendingInbox` ranks branded above pooled precisely on that meaning. **Lying to the schema to defeat a constraint is how the next reader is misled**, and the constraint was right: it was describing a model we had drifted from without noticing. **#610 minted 🔴.**
- **4 Aug (🔥 THE WARMUP CLOCK IS RUNNING — the launch blocker moved for the first time in a month):** **4 Google mailboxes across 2 domains are warming in Instantly Growth.** `jacques@` + `hello@` on both `kindoutreach.com` and `trykind.org`, every one authenticated (**MX · DKIM 2048 · SPF · DMARC**) *before* warmup began — because warming a domain that fails authentication burns the whole three weeks and you find out at the end. **Clock: 4 Aug → warm ~25 Aug**, which is the date #553's send ladder counts from, and it lands inside the founder's 31 Aug outside edge. **THE ONE DELIBERATE DISAGREEMENT WITH THE VENDOR:** Instantly suggests a daily warmup cap of **10**; we set **25**. Warmup volume should approximate real send volume — a box warmed to 10/day has built reputation for 10/day, and the jump to ~30 real cold sends is precisely the spike that gets a new domain flagged. At +1/day it still climbs gently over the same three weeks. Recorded because it is a judgement call against a vendor default, and if deliverability disappoints this is the first number to revisit. **THE CONNECTION IS OAUTH, NOT APP PASSWORD, AND THAT WAS A REVERSAL OF MY OWN ADVICE.** I first told the founder to use the App Password *"so Instantly connects the same way our engine does"* — **wrong**: the two connections are independent, so parity buys nothing, while Instantly's own note that app passwords are *"more prone to disconnects"* buys a real risk. A silent disconnect does not pause warmup, it stops it, and nobody would notice for a week. OAuth was blocked by a Workspace default until we configured **Instantly's client ID as a Trusted app** under Security → API controls — **org-level, so one config covered both domains**, correcting Instantly's own *"once per domain"* wording. **The App Passwords are not wasted:** they are what our OWN engine uses at step 7. **A dead end recorded so it is not re-walked:** the App Password route failed with Instantly's generic *"unable to connect"*; I diagnosed IMAP-disabled and had the founder check the admin setting — **IMAP was already ON**, so the diagnosis was wrong and the real cause was most likely that the mailboxes were minutes old and still propagating. Asking him to look before changing anything is what kept that from becoming a wrong "fix". **⚠️ NO CAMPAIGNS IN INSTANTLY, EVER** — it is a warmup utility; a campaign there sends outside our engine, our approval gate, our opt-out list and our record. **#198 flipped 🔴 → 🩷** — the first movement on the #1 priority since June. It is not 🟢 until a real send lands in a real inbox (#553).
- **4 Aug (📮 BOTH COLD DOMAINS FULLY SET UP — and counting the rows found a domain with NO DMARC at all):** walked the founder through Google Workspace end to end, one screen at a time. **`kindoutreach.com` and `trykind.org` are both verified**, each carrying the complete deliverability set: **MX → `SMTP.GOOGLE.COM` pri 1 · DKIM 2048-bit at `google._domainkey` · SPF `include:_spf.google.com` · DMARC `p=none` reporting to the founder.** That is the plumbing most cold-email setups get wrong, in place *before* a single mailbox exists — which matters because warming a domain that fails authentication wastes the entire three weeks. **THE CATCH WORTH KEEPING: GoDaddy PRE-SEEDS a `_dmarc` record on every domain** (`p=quarantine`, reports to `dmarc_rua@onsecureserver.net`). We added ours alongside it, so `kindoutreach.com` ended with **two `_dmarc` rows — which means NO DMARC at all**, because a domain publishing two is ignored entirely. It saved cleanly, showed no error, and would have looked correct forever. Found only by asking the founder to **count the rows** rather than trust the save. On `trykind.org` we edited GoDaddy's row instead and it was right first time. **Rule for any future domain: EDIT the existing `_dmarc`, never add a second** — and GoDaddy's `p=quarantine` default would junk our own mail while a new domain is still proving itself. **Three other things surfaced and were handled:** a **red billing warning** (*"issue with your billing account… suspension in 13 days"*) which the founder fixed — unaddressed it would have killed the account mid-warmup; the Workspace admin was auto-created as **`www@kindoutreach.com`**, a domain prefix rather than a name and unusable as a sender identity, rename to `jacques@` pending; and the founder's **personal Gmail, Drive and Calendar are unaffected** — verified against Google's own warning banner, which names only Family, Fit, Nest and One. **Free trial to 18 Aug 2026.** *(Runbook: step 4 complete. Steps 1–2 still deferred by the founder; step 5 — the four mailboxes — is next and is where billing starts.)*

It had sat unticked since Day 0 while being described in the docs as load-bearing. **The screenshot carried a second fact worth more than the tick:** *"Multi-region replicas are only available on the Pro plan."* On the current plan the replica count **cannot** be raised — so the risk is not merely closed, it is structurally shut, and the guard is the billing tier rather than anyone's discipline. **Recorded with its own expiry: re-check the day Railway goes Pro**, because that is the moment the guard silently disappears. *(Steps 1 and 2 of the founder's runbook — the $0 money walk and the fresh-signup re-test — deferred by him, not skipped; they stay open on the board.)*

A test you cannot afford is not a gate; it is a queue.
- **4 Aug (🚨 THE FOUNDER'S TEST SIGNUP FOUND TWO LIVE BUGS AND DISPROVED A RECORDED CLAIM — this is why the walk exists):** he created a fresh client to run the $299 test purchase and hit both defects in one pass. **① Signup was DEAD for every new client, for two days.** #607 writes `current_period_end: null` deliberately (a billing period on a non-billing row fed the old at-risk arithmetic) — but the **live schema has NOT NULL on that column**, the relaxing migration cannot run, and the insert died 23502 at `auth.ts`. The LAUNCH-PAD claim *"the code was deliberately written to work with or without the migration"* was **false** — nothing had tested the row against a schema that refuses null, and the founder was the first person to sign up since #607 shipped. Fix: **honest null first, one retry with a 2099 sentinel** (`PERIOD_END_SENTINEL` — far-future because a realistic date would flag every dormant signup "at risk" the day it passed; 2099 is already the repo's far-future idiom). The day the constraint relaxes, the honest write starts succeeding and the sentinel branch goes dormant — no second deploy. **② The onboarding button still said "go live for $99"** — one line above small print correctly reading $299, both on the screen where a client decides. The 3-Aug sweep fixed the small print and missed the button: **grep found the file and reading fixed the wrong half of it.** Now interpolated like everything else. **③ His payment-page screenshot showed "K.I.N.D wallet top-up · $299"** — not a new bug: #1257 (the label fix) was merged but not yet shipped; the amount was right and the purchase safe. **The pattern across all three:** every one was found by a human walking the real path with real state — the gate is necessary and not sufficient, which is what 🩷-before-🟢 has claimed all along. **#607 #414.**
- **4 Aug (🔎 THE STRIPE BLOCKER WAS SMALLER THAN I CLAIMED — corrected by reading the code instead of repeating myself):** preparing the founder's Stripe walkthrough, I re-read `lib/stripe.ts` before writing the steps — and the 3-Aug claim that *"the product cannot take a payment until the $299 price exists in Stripe"* is **false**. The pack checkout uses **inline `price_data`** (`stripe.ts:113-132`): the amount is passed at runtime and the API derives it from `PACK_PRICE_USD`, so **the checkout has been charging $299 correctly since #1253 deployed**. No price to create, no env var to point. The #414 "checkout renders the dashboard product" rule is real but belongs to the **dormant subscription checkout** — a different code path. I applied the wrong checkout's rule to the money path and put a false blocker at the top of the founder's board for a day. **What actually remains in Stripe is cosmetic-but-real:** the dashboard product description pasted on 3 Aug still says **$99** and needs the $299 wording, and the **$299 test purchase** is still owed. The lesson is the session's only content: **I wrote the 3-Aug block from memory of #414 instead of reading the function it described. A rule remembered from a different context is not a fact about this one.** *(Same day: Google Workspace verified early — the mailbox purchase unblocked a day ahead of schedule.)*
- **3 Aug (💵 THE $299 SIGN-UP — the door stopped costing the founder money, and the price move nearly broke the till):** founder-locked, superseding his own 24–25 Jul $99 lock. **The reasoning is the record:** at $99 he funded **~$78 of every engine-acquired client** — CAC **~$143** (Apollo $65 + verification $8 + AI writing $70) against month-one setup **~$134** — and **carried the churn bet personally**: a client who quit after month one cost him $78 in cash, every time. At **$299** the client funds their own acquisition, their own setup and his onboarding hours; day-one position is **+$18.50 even after full CAC**, and a quitter costs nothing. **He chose the list price deliberately over $199** (which covered setup but left half of CAC exposed) on one argument: *you can always discount down from $299 in your own mouth; you cannot quietly raise from $199 without punishing the early clients who took the risk on you.* **Discounts are founder discretion applied by hand in Stripe — never in code, on the site, or in the product.** **TWO COSTING ERRORS SURFACED WHILE PRICING IT, and both had been making the old economics look survivable.** The model costed a client's warmed inbox at **$4.50/month** when a pre-warmed Smartlead mailbox is **$45/MONTH** — wrong by a factor of ten — and carried a **$40/month** per-client workspace fee that was **never confirmed with the vendor** and is really **~$8** (their own Google box plus domain). One number too low, one too high, and between them the $99 read as viable. **And a correction I owed the founder in writing:** I told him warmth *cannot be bought from anyone*. **That was wrong** — Smartlead sells pre-warmed mailboxes and he had confirmed the $45 price himself. I said it with confidence and without checking, which is the same failure as the *"verified"* $138 servers line earlier the same day. What **is** true, and survives: **every mailbox starts cold**, so *pre-warmed* only ever means somebody began warming it weeks earlier — which is why the $45 is **month one only**, with the client's own branded box warming alongside and outreach transitioning at **~day 21–29** (the 1 Jul onboarding lock, its branded leg updated to our Google-direct rig so our engine holds the password). **THE DEFECT THE PRICE MOVE EXPOSED, and it would have been silent:** `routes/stripe.ts` hardcoded `const FIRST_PURCHASE_USD = 99` while the portal's button already derived from `PACK_PRICE_USD` — #563 fixed the client side and left the server side typed. Moving the constant alone would have had Milla POST **299** and the API reject it as `first_purchase_must_be_99`: **every first payment failing at the till**, on the single request that turns a signup into a client, with no symptom but a 400 nobody was watching. Nothing in 1,601 tests would have gone red — the portal test passes because it derives, the constant test passes because 99 became 299. **A single-source rule enforced on one side of a two-sided handshake is not enforced.** Both sides now derive and `pack-price-single-source.test.ts` fails if either is re-hardcoded. **The gate caught two more of my own losses:** my rewrite of the checkout description **dropped *"Reviewing is free"***, which `pricing-copy.test.ts` pins as *"the single most misread thing about the model"* — a client who believes browsing costs money never browses — restored rather than loosened; and my restore-after-red-proof edited the **comment quoting the code instead of the code**, because the comment contains the line it describes. **Fifth time that trap has fired in this repo.** **Left deliberately undone:** the marketing offer now says the client's domain and mailbox are *"yours to keep"* and that onboarding includes training — **neither was written into the Terms**, because ownership-transfer-on-churn is an obligation nobody has decided (the domain sits on our registrar, the mailbox on our Workspace) and training is a service level. Flagged in `terms.html` for the founder and counsel. **Also fixed there:** §3 said *"No payment is taken at onboarding"* while §4 said work begins when the pack lands — **a contract contradicting itself one page apart**. **#609.**
- **3 Aug (💸 THE FLOOR WAS WRONG BY $87/MO, AND THE "VERIFIED" TAG IS WHY IT SURVIVED):** the founder ran the numbers and said ***"i cant afford 470/month… without income coming in this is impossible to maintain"***. Cutting was the obvious response; **checking was the right one first**. The cashflow model's *Servers + database* line read **$138** and carried a green **verified** tag. He opened the actual invoices: **Railway $15.59 + Supabase $35 = $50.59**. Nothing had changed at either vendor — the figure had **never been checked against a bill**, and the confident tag is precisely what stopped anyone re-checking it. **A confident label on an unread number is worse than no number at all**, and it is the same defect the 29 Jun audit rule exists for, committed by the model that rule is written into. **Then the cuts, and what matters is what was NOT cut.** Resend → **free tier** ($20 saved; ⚠️ ~3,000/mo and **~100/day** is a ceiling that bites the day clients arrive) · Apollo → **free plan from 3 Sep** ($65 saved, with **2,500 already-paid credits** covering August's prospecting) · Claude Code **downgraded** (~$152 → ~$23, current plan to 20 Aug) · the **$12 failover teardown re-prioritised**, since $12 reads differently under cost pressure than it did as a lazy-afternoon job. **Google Workspace ($28) and Instantly Growth ($37) were deliberately kept** — cutting the sending path saves ~$65 and pushes first revenue further away, which is the actual problem. **~$470 → ~$157/mo all-in; product floor ~$146, ~$134 once the failover dies.** The number that reframes it: at $4 per approved lead, **~$134/mo is ~34 approvals — roughly ONE client.** **One process failure worth recording:** I did this work while `HEAD` sat on `claude/client-zero-arch`, **37 commits behind main**, and only caught it because the doc-lint dot counts came back as Σ577 when main says Σ572. **The lint caught what I did not.** Verified the base file was byte-identical before re-applying to a clean branch. Second time in two days a stale ref has bitten — **verify the ref before working from it, not after.**
- **3 Aug (📮 THE MAILBOXES ARE BOUGHT — the launch blocker moved for the first time in a month, and it is now waiting on Google, not on us):** worked steps 4–8 one at a time at the founder's request (*"this is critical… i never done this before"*). **Step 4 passed: `INBOX_SECRET_KEY` is set** — and *how* it was verified is the part worth keeping: there is **no green tick**, the Vida → Engine screen shows a red *"Nothing can send"* box **only on failure**, so **absence is the pass**. `inbox-secret.ts` demands exactly 64 hex chars and **fails closed** — and rotating the key makes every stored mailbox password undecryptable, so it must never be regenerated. **Step 5 half-done: two cold-send domains bought** — `kindoutreach.com` and `trykind.org`, **£20.45 one-off at GoDaddy** — with Google Workspace signed up on the `.com` as primary and the `.org` to be added as a **secondary domain in the same subscription**, one bill instead of two. **Google's verification takes ~48h, so steps 5 and 6 are parked until ~5 Aug**; the blocker is now external. Neither existing domain could do this job: `get-kind.com` carries the invoices (a spam complaint there poisons the business) and `gettingkind.com` **has no mailbox at all** — it is Resend send-plus-inbound-webhook, with no password to give the engine. **Step 7 closed and a recorded number corrected: Apollo is Basic Monthly at $65/mo**, not the **$49** this repo has said since 1 Aug — 2,500 credits a month, **0 used**, renewing 3 Sep. Both facts kept rather than reconciled; **$65 is the number to budget**, and the unused credits are prospecting already paid for. **TECH-STACK's Apollo row still said "retired"** — the exact wording the founder overruled on 1 Aug, surviving in a second doc — now rewritten to *OUR hunting*, with the audit's altitude slip recorded on the row so it cannot be re-made. **And the LAUNCH-PAD contradicted itself:** six founder actions were written up as done in its prose while their checkboxes still read ⬜ — a doc disagreeing with itself is worse than a doc that is merely stale, because both halves look authoritative. Ticked. **The teaching note:** the founder said *"you have confused me so much"*, and he was right — I had been answering in field names and file paths for a walkthrough he had told me he had never done before. **A correct answer pitched at the wrong altitude is a wrong answer.**
- **3 Aug (🧭 FOUR FOUNDER ACTIONS OFF THE BOARD — and the monitor nearly got built so it could never fail):** worked steps 9–12 together, one at a time. **#199 production monitoring is live** — and the setup detail is the whole item: `/health` **always returns 200 by design** so Railway's deploy check passes, with the database's real state in the *body*. A plain HTTP monitor would therefore have **sat green through a total database outage** — failing at the one job it was bought for. Built as a **keyword** monitor alerting when `"status":"ok"` stops appearing, which catches both an unreachable API and a live-but-degraded one. Until today nothing watched production and the first to know would have been a client. **#317 — refund and chargeback handling can run for the first time.** The code shipped months ago at `stripe.ts:772+` and was reachable only by events Stripe had never been told to send, so a refund or chargeback left the client holding the credits: we lost the money AND the leads. The endpoint now subscribes all three. **#414 closed the half no code could reach** — the sentence a client reads as their card is charged comes from the Stripe *dashboard* product, not from `PRICING`; the repo's copy was fixed in July and the dashboard was always the founder's to edit. **`PAYSTACK_SECRET_KEY` deleted from Railway** (`FLUTTERWAVE_*` already gone with #314) — a live secret for a processor removed in #352 was sitting in the environment for no reason. **The failover teardown was PARKED, deliberately and with the founder's agreement.** It is $12/mo against a launch blocked on mailboxes, and its first step risks dropping the portal mid-DNS-edit. **What makes parking it safe is that #199 landed the same day**: an outage is now *noticed* in 5 minutes even though it is no longer *auto-routed around* — and the failover was never tested anyway. **GoDaddy closed with no action**, correctly: it is the registrar, Cloudflare holds DNS, and the two live domains must never be touched. **One process note worth keeping:** mid-session I read a stale `origin/main` and briefly concluded a #606 fix had not landed. It had. **A checkout is a claim like any other — verify the ref before reporting from it.**

The purchase-order table is marked **❓ unknown** on every line rather than implying the founder has done things this container cannot see. **KIND-MASTER's RESUME-HERE block — the one a fresh session is told to read first — said *"Last updated 25 Jun · Product LAUNCHED 18 Jun · TWO DOCS ONLY"***. Coaching stays as-is and stays free — a retention lever, not a SKU. **#602 recorded · #608.**
- **1 Aug (💰 #349's REMAINDER — 11 more swallowed money writes, and the scanner had to be rebuilt twice before its number could be trusted):** the first pass reported **61** unchecked writes. Reading them showed it was conflating adjacent statements — a `const { data } = await db.from('x')` read, then an unrelated `.insert(` twelve lines below. Rewritten with real statement extraction it said **35**, and reading THOSE found `admin.ts` was a false positive, already guarded by the 29 Jul pass. **Reporting 61 would have been the exact failure this repo keeps punishing: a number produced by a tool nobody checked.** **Three could cost a client real money.** ① **Both Stripe ledger rollbacks:** the row is deleted so Stripe's retry can re-grant, and the replay is idempotent *on that reference* — so a swallowed delete means the retry sees the row, concludes the grant already happened, and skips it. **The client has paid and is never credited, permanently**, while the alert says *"Stripe will retry"*. ② **The referral payout's atomic claim:** an errored UPDATE left `claimed` undefined, indistinguishable from losing the race, so the function returned and **the referrer was simply never paid** — every other step in that function alerts loudly, the one deciding whether they run did not. Topical, since the same day's #327 work corrected the page that promises that $45. ③ **`credit-holds.ts:55`** — the compensating credit return was swallowed **one line below a comment promising *"return the credit so we never silently swallow it"***. If it fails the credit is gone and no hold exists to reconcile it against: the client paid for work that was never held, and the log said *"credit returned"* regardless. **A category I had not expected: rollbacks that make alerts LIE.** Three referral rollbacks swallowed their errors while the alert asserted *"the payout marker was reset"* whether or not it had been. A failed reset leaves the marker set, so no future purchase retries — and the operator has been told the opposite. **An alert that misreports is worse than silence**, because it ends the investigation. Each now reports what actually happened. **And one that is not money but is felt by a person:** `reply-pipeline`'s `status:'replied'` write. Swallowed, the enrolment stays active and the next cron **emails somebody who has already replied to our client**. **The remainder is stated, not implied: 24 sites left, deliberately** — 8 in seed/demo builders where no client's money exists, the rest low-consequence markers. Listed in the PR so the next person inherits a boundary rather than a guess. **The rule the sweep worked to, worth keeping:** a checked write is only finished when the failure branch says what it **costs**. *"The client has paid and will never be credited"* is actionable at 2am; *"insert failed"* is not. 8 tests, red-proven twice.
- **1 Aug (📜 THE PAPER PASS — and the worst line on the site was one none of the three items named):** #413/#410/#327 swept together with the founder's explicit P12 approval for the pages named in the prompt. **Most of what the three items describe was already fixed** — terms §5/§6 match the code exactly, the legal pages already name PDL + Hunter with no Flutterwave or Paystack, and **two of #327's citations point at things that no longer exist**: `pricing.html:491`'s *"HubSpot & Salesforce integration"* was removed in a July sweep, and **`virtual-assistant.html` was deleted outright** in the July FIGSY-cut (`ae8d5ca9`, 37 pages) before the *nothing gets deleted* lock. Reported as NOT-POSSIBLE rather than quietly skipped. **WHAT THE SWEEP ACTUALLY FOUND was the signup modal, on NINE pages:** *"Free to start — you only pay per qualified lead. No card required."* **Three falsehoods in one sentence, rendered at the exact moment a person decides to sign up** — there is no free start (the first purchase is $99), *"per qualified lead"* is the retired 8-Jul ladder, and a card **is** required. **It survived every previous sweep because it is assigned inside a `<script>` block** (`getElementById('auth-sub').textContent`), so a visual walk and a grep over rendered text both miss it. That is the lesson worth keeping: the claim a buyer reads is not always in the HTML you can see. **#413's real defect was one clause:** terms said *"if your balance falls below $4, approvals pause"* — **false for the first 100.** `approve-lead.ts` only returns `insufficient_funds` once `pack.left` hits 0; during the pack nothing touches the wallet and a zero balance stops nothing. Reading the charge path settled it, not the item. It is a contract, so exactness is the point. **#410 needed a reframing, not a removal:** all three legal pages called Apollo *"used only where a Client configures it"*, which reads as part of the standard client stack against the 30 Jul lock (*Apollo is OUR hunting only*). The BYO-key path does exist, so the **disclosure stays** — removing it would under-disclose if a client ever supplied a key — but it now says whose tool it is. **Two existing tests failed on the rewording and both were pinned to sentences rather than meanings** — one demanded the literal word *"configure"* while the new copy qualifies Apollo more strongly, the other demanded an exact CRM sentence whose claim was unchanged. Broadened to assert the claim. **A third guard I wrote was too blunt and caught itself:** it flagged `figsy.html` for naming Paystack and Flutterwave, which on reading are **fictional prospects at real Nigerian fintechs** in a mock lead table — a company name as a prospect's employer is not a disclosure about our payment stack. Scoped to the legal pages instead. 12 tests, red-proven. `scripts/freeze-website.sh` re-run in the same PR per P12. **13 pages changed, all listed in the PR. Client-facing → preview first.** **#413 #410 #327 🔴→🟡.**
- **1 Aug (🧹 #406 PORTAL SWEEP — four lies removed, and the worst was a number we invented for the client):** #406 says *every* screen in `apps/portal`. That is **104 pages, and a client can reach 19** — `middleware.ts` bounces every signed-in client off `/dashboard/*` to `/milla/*`, so a dead button on a retired page is not a lie told to anyone. **The scope decision IS the finding:** ten of the nineteen are `(dashboard)` files rendered inside **13-line Milla wrappers** — the client's real screen, reached by import rather than by URL, which is exactly how they escaped the previous sweep. **The worst: `analytics/page.tsx` fabricated the client's unsubscribe count** — `Math.round(m.replies * 0.05)`, on a chart they can select, **one line below a comment saying *"never fabricate a bounce number"***. The old code computed the month and then ignored it: it could never have matched, because `month` arrives as a display label (*"Jan 26"*) and never equals a `YYYY-MM` slice — so it gave up and invented five percent. Now counted honestly by the API, the only place holding the month key. **The referral page promised a currency we retired:** *"15 FIGSY credits"* ×3, the work wallet of the two-wallet model, while `stripe.ts` pays **$45 into the one wallet** and says so in its own comment. The backend was migrated and the page never was — a client could refer someone and be paid in a different currency from the one promised. It also told them their referral *"starts their free trial"*, retired hours earlier by #607. **It under-sold, too:** $45 is truer and better than a credit count nobody can price. **A dead notification bell** sat on every Milla screen with no click, no badge and nothing behind it, and the portal's **last `alert()`** was doing duty as a feature. Both replaced with the disabled+labelled pattern Settings and Billing already use. **What the sweep CLEARED matters as much:** the milla native pages have **no dead buttons at all**, Settings' and Billing's unbuilt toggles are properly disabled, KPIs' LinkedIn block is an honest *Coming soon*, and ROI omits what it cannot source. Prior honesty work holds up; what it missed was the wrapped tree. **8 tests, red-proven three ways** — and they strip comments first, because the first run failed four times **on the notes explaining the removals**. A guard that cannot tell a live string from an explanation of its removal forces the next person to delete the reasoning along with the code; same lesson #607 hit hours earlier, same shared stripper. **Client-facing → preview first. #406 🔴→🟡.**
- **1 Aug (🧹 #397 CLOSED — the dead HubSpot code is gone, and the item that described it was the more dangerous half):** #397 said *"platform signup/payment sync functions written but never called (`lib/hubspot.ts`). Wire or delete."* **Two of the five exports are live** — `syncFigsyInterestedToHubspot` (imported `reply-pipeline.ts:34`, **called** `:278`) and `getHubspotPipelineView` (imported `internal.ts:25`, **called** `:2581`) — so the cheap branch of that instruction deletes code that runs when a prospect replies. **And it would have failed silently:** both call sites are best-effort `.catch(console.error)`, so the result is a quiet gap in CRM data, not an error anyone sees. That is why the premise was corrected on the row (#606) before a line was touched. **Three exports really were dead** and are removed (458 → 265 lines), each proved by reading every mention of the name across every app and package tree — definition plus its own error log, no importer, no dynamic import, no call site: `syncNewSignupToHubspot` (**a signup has never reached HubSpot**), `syncPaymentToHubspot` (never called from `stripe.ts`; it also carried the hardcoded `amountZar / 19` ZAR→USD fossil #352 removed from the Paystack path), and `syncClientToHubspot` — whose **only** caller was the signup sync, so keeping it would have created NEW dead code, the exact state the item was filed about. **The trap in the middle:** `upsertContact` and `addNoteToContact` were shared by the dead syncs AND the live one. A cleanup that followed the callers out would have left `syncFigsyInterestedToHubspot` exported and broken — the failure mode being fixed, reintroduced by fixing it. They stay, and a test asserts the live export still calls them. **9 tests pin both live exports to their importers AND their call sites** — asserting the import alone would not catch "imported but unused", which is precisely what #397 claimed — plus a source-tree walk that fails if a removed name reappears. Red-proven three ways: deleting the live export, dropping the call while keeping the import, re-adding a dead name. **Two other docs repeated the false claim as an illustration and were corrected** (#573's *reachable ≠ used* example, #327's website-claims row) — one owner per fact, and the example is now stronger for being true: hubspot.ts is core, 3 of 5 exports were dead, 2 run on the reply path. **#397 → 🩷.**
- **1 Aug (🛑 THE TRIAL IS RETIRED — it had no place in the money model and it was still emailing real people):** the founder ruled kill. Every signup wrote `status:'trialing'` with a 14-day `trial_ends_at`, and two crons acted on it daily — **one of them sending *"Your K.I.N.D trial ends in 4 days… Subscribe now →"*** with a button to a billing page, about a product we have not sold since 24 Jul. **Signup now writes `paused`** — the client exists, holds the entitlement, and is dormant until the $99 lands — with `trial_ends_at` and `current_period_end` NULL, because those two fields are precisely what made "4 days left" computable by anything reading the row later. **The status choice was constrained, not stylistic:** `subscriptions.status` is a Postgres ENUM and `auth.ts` throws on a failed insert, so an invented value like "dormant" would have **500'd every signup** — #342's exact shape, still live elsewhere in this repo. Of the five provable enum values, `active` would have booked every unpaid signup as revenue (#340 deliberately), `past_due`/`cancelled` misdescribe what happened, and `paused` is the only one whose plain meaning is the founder's own sentence. **Reading rather than grepping paid twice.** ① `/ae/nurture` **never read `trialing`** — it read `clients.created_at` inside 14 days, so retiring the status alone would have left it telling every new signup *"Your K.I.N.D trial is live"*; and it was the **one lifecycle cron with no `lifecycleEmailsEnabled()` gate**, so the #480 kill-switch never covered it. ② The lead-drip's trial-halt accepted an active sub, an in-period trial, or a past purchase — **our own comped house client (#600) has only the trial row**, so retiring the trial and leaving that halt would have silently stopped Client Zero's drip on day 15.
