- **29 Jul (⏰ #491 — a dead cron was silent, and the hard part was the throttle):** `callInternal` already wrote every run to `cron_runs` and dead-lettered every failure — and **neither of those is a signal.** Both are PULL surfaces: true the moment they are written, read only when somebody thinks to look. So a job that started failing kept failing on schedule and the first person to find out would be the client whose leads stopped arriving. **Two detectors, because a scheduled job stops in two genuinely different ways:** ① it RAN AND FAILED, which leaves an `ok=false` row; and ② it **NEVER RAN AT ALL**, which leaves nothing for ① to fire on — that is the **claim-then-crash path #343 itself introduced**: a process claims the slot (the claim is an INSERT, so the slot is now taken), then dies before finishing, every other replica *correctly* stands down, and the job simply does not happen. The single-run guard working exactly as designed, producing a silent miss. Same shape as a dead scheduler or a `RUN_CRONS=false` left on by accident. **The alert was the easy half; the throttle was the work.** `/figsy/send-due-all` runs every two hours, so alerting on each failure sends twelve identical emails a day — and an alert that arrives twelve times is an alert that gets filtered, which is precisely how the NEXT real one is missed (the same reasoning that keeps `smartleadRefusalIsNews` quiet on its five expected refusals). Three rules that pull against each other, held at once: a failure **after a success** always alerts (a job that was working and now is not is the most actionable signal there is) · an **ongoing** outage stays quiet for 24h · but it **nags again** once the window passes, so silence is never mistaken for recovery. **No new infra, per the brief:** the throttle marker rides in `cron_runs.note`, which is already written and already read by the health page — and deliberately not an in-memory counter, because this process restarts on every deploy, which is exactly when a cron is most likely to be broken. **The stale detector measures each job's own median gap instead of carrying a list of "daily jobs":** a list lives in one file while the schedule lives in another and nobody updates two places, and it would have fired on all four genuinely weekly jobs — four false alarms being how an alert channel dies. Measured cadence means the weeklies exclude themselves and an hourly job that stops is caught with no configuration at all. It runs inside the existing daily prune job rather than a new timer, because a new scheduled process is one more thing that can itself die silently. **And the branch that matters most: a history read failure ALERTS rather than staying quiet** — unable to evaluate the throttle must mean "tell them", never "say nothing". 25 tests, **red proven** by deleting the throttle and the weekly exclusion (3 fail, including the weekly false-alarm guard). **1054 across 66 files** (1029/65 before), gate 6/6 green.
- **29 Jul (📮 #547 — the last shared sender to a real prospect, and a decision that was overdue):** #547 moved every sequence step onto the client's own mailbox and **left `manual-reply.ts:54` on `COLD_FROM`**. So the state it shipped into was worse than either extreme: the machine-written steps left the client's mailbox and the **human reply in the same conversation left the shared address** — the prospect watched the sender change mid-thread, and every client's manual replies pooled their spam complaints onto one domain, which is RULEBOOK 12.2 exactly. It now runs the same resolve-or-refuse as the sequence path. **The refusal is the feature:** a 409 carrying the operator-readable reason, because a human clicked Send and is owed the sentence — and because a silent fallback looks like success while the damage lands on every other client weeks later, where nobody connects it back. The reasoning is written into `email.ts`, not just here. ⚠️ **One sub-question left open on purpose:** consent mail leaves from the **transactional** identity while D4 forbids cold-adjacent mail poisoning that domain — moving it touches the domain every invoice and password reset uses, so it gets its own PR and the founder's call. **And a measurement correction worth keeping:** I have twice quoted suite totals from an `apps/api`-scoped run while calling them the gate's. - **27 Jul (📬 #551 — REPLIES LAND BACK AGAINST THE RIGHT INBOX, built BEFORE the defect can bite):** the chain is now **inbox → client → lead → thread**. `InboundReply` carries `toEmail`, `resolveInboxOwner()` maps `client_inboxes.email` → client, and `routeReply()` keeps only that client's matches. **The point worth holding on to is WHY this had to be built now:** today every reply arrives at one shared Resend inbox, so matching on the PROSPECT'S address and fanning out to every client holding that lead is **correct** — that is R1, and narrowing it would have started dropping replies the day it shipped. The moment a client sends from their own mailbox the reply arrives **there**, and the same fan-out becomes **one client reading another client's inbound mail**. So the fix is not a change of behaviour today; it is the routing being ready the hour the first mailbox is recorded, and a test asserts that an unknown inbox behaves **exactly as it does now**. **A known inbox with no matching lead returns EMPTY and alerts — it does NOT fall back**, because falling back is precisely the harm: handing a stranger's reply at Acme's mailbox to whichever other client happens to hold that lead. The status filter is **deliberately absent**: a released mailbox still receives mail for weeks and the pooled→branded handover overlaps by design, so filtering to `active` would silently orphan the tail of every switched-over client. A lookup failure falls back to the fan-out — an outage may cost precision, never a reply. **Second clause, and it is the one that would have bitten silently:** idempotency keyed on `svix-id`, which is a **Resend transport id**. Instantly and Smartlead send no such header, so the moment a second provider delivered replies the guard would key on an empty string, `isDuplicateWebhookEvent` would fail open on **every** event, and a retried hot reply would re-run the whole path — a second CRM deal, a second alert, a second counter bump. `replyEventKey()` prefers the provider's own message id and **namespaces it by provider**, because two providers can legitimately issue the same id and neither must ever suppress the other's reply. 25 tests, red proven by breaking the unknown-inbox fallback and the namespace. 997 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (📮 PROMPT 7 — SMARTLEAD FOR CLIENTS, THE EXACT MIRROR OF INSTANTLY, and nothing exercised against a live workspace):** built to the same three-file shape — `smartlead.ts` Phase 2 (create campaign · save sequence · add leads, appended below the untouched Phase-1 read-only client), `smartlead-map.ts` (pure gates + payloads) and `smartlead-send.ts` (the hand-off) — **and wired into `approve-lead.ts` in the SAME PR**, because Prompt 4's lesson was a client and a mapping that nothing called. **The property that matters is that the two gates are exact INVERSES:** `canPushToInstantly` refuses anything that is NOT the house client, `canPushToSmartlead` refuses anything that IS, and a unit test asserts *for any client, at most one route accepts*. That is what lets the money path attempt **both** pushes with no if/else — deliberately, because an if/else would put the routing decision in the money path where a future edit could quietly send a client's lead from our mailboxes; the mutual exclusion lives in the gate functions where it is tested. **Four things reading the real files changed:** ① `SequenceStep` has **`wait_days`, not `day`** — days since the PREVIOUS step, which is exactly Smartlead's `delay_in_days`, so it passes straight through while Instantly's cumulative `day` has to be accumulated; getting that backwards fires a whole sequence at once or stretches it over months. ② **Non-email steps are dropped**, or a LinkedIn step becomes an email to a real prospect in our client's name. ③ The sequence is saved **only on campaign create** — re-saving per approval overwrites the copy mid-flight and a prospect halfway through receives step 2 of a different email. ④ **A `smartlead.ts` already existed** (Phase-1 read-only, June) and I nearly overwrote it: it was read first and EXTENDED, and it carried real endpoint detail (`/email-accounts/?offset=0&limit=100`) that no doc page would give me. **Security detail specific to this provider:** Smartlead authenticates by **query parameter**, so the credential is in every URL and lands in any log that records one — `redact()` therefore scrubs the **URL-encoded** form as well, because a key containing `+` or `/` appears as `%2B`/`%2F` and slips past a plain replace. `ignore_global_block_list` and `ignore_unsubscribe_list` are both **false**: setting them true to get more leads through is emailing people who said stop. **NOT_POSSIBLE names four limits**, chief among them that the **sequence STEP shape is UNVERIFIED** — api.smartlead.ai and helpcenter.smartlead.ai both return 403 to this environment, exactly as developer.instantly.ai did — so the endpoint is confirmed and the field names are not, and that must be checked against a live workspace **before the first client send**. **53 tests**, red proven by deleting the house-client refusal (4 red, including *"NEVER BOTH — for any client, at most one route accepts"*). One of my own assertions read `out[0].body` where the field is `email_body`: `undefined` does not contain `{{`, so it **would have passed while asserting nothing** — caught only because the same test also checked the rendered text. 972 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🧾 THE THREE OWED ITEMS CLOSED — plus a placeholder of mine that reached production):** carried since the Prompt 5 audit; each is the same defect in different clothes — a screen built to tell the truth telling something slightly untrue about itself. **① The ledger integrity row told the founder to run a migration he had already run.** It asked *"does the live ledger accept the wallet types?"* and answered by looking for a ROW of that type; none yet → *"CANNOT tell … run 20260726_wallet_tx_types."* He had run it, repeatedly. **It could not tell because it was asking the wrong question:** *has a row ever been written* is about HISTORY, *will the constraint accept this* is about the SCHEMA — and Postgres answers that directly from `pg_constraint`, before any payment exists. Now reads the constraint definition itself, with the row-based reading kept only as a fallback for when `pg_catalog` is unreachable. **A false instruction that is correctly ignored teaches you to ignore the screen**, which is worse than saying nothing. **② A 4xx from a MISSING route read as "answering".** The endpoint check counted any 2xx-or-4xx as healthy, which cannot distinguish *the route declined your request* from *there is no such route* — delete a route and Express's default handler returns `Cannot GET /operator/x`, reported **green**. All twenty routes were verified to exist, so it was right **by luck rather than by construction**, on the screen whose entire purpose is not being right by luck. Our routes answer JSON, Express's fallback answers HTML: that is the tell, and it is now the check. `/blockers`, `/bookings` and `/nexus` also gained the notes they never had — *"HTTP 404 — answering."* with nothing explaining why a 404 was acceptable is **a green the reader cannot check**. **③ The reply path was not on the System screen at all.** The check covered signup → pay → source → approve → send and stopped — yet a reply is the thing the client is BUYING, and the one step they cannot see failing, because *a reply that never arrives looks exactly like a prospect who never answered*. Three sessions of work went into that spine and none of it was reported. The new row is CHECKED-BROKEN when `RESEND_WEBHOOK_SECRET` is unset (every inbound reply rejected, silently) and explicitly says **configured is not the same as proven**. **④ And my own mistake.** I wrote a worked example for rewriting `DATABASE_URL`, with a placeholder ref, and it was pasted in literally — production came back `(ENOTFOUND) tenant/user postgres.abcdefghijk not found`, Supavisor being perfectly accurate and completely unhelpful: **it names a tenant, not a mistake.** `DATABASE_URL` and `SUPABASE_URL` sit beside each other in Railway and must describe the SAME project, so a disagreement is **always** a typo — now detected before any connection attempt, in all four paths that read the database, with one sentence naming the variable, the wrong value and the right one. Blast radius was checked before advising: `DATABASE_URL` is used only by the migration runner and the three admin probes, so **no client-facing path was affected.** 22 tests. 919 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🛑 CI HAS NEVER RUN. NOT ONCE. - **27 Jul (💾 #298 — THE DRILL THAT CANNOT BE RUN, AND THE PIECE THAT WAS MISSING ANYWAY):** plan written; **the drill itself remains undone and the document says so in its first line.** A restore drill is *take a backup → restore it somewhere → prove it matches*, and the middle step is impossible: the **Supabase dashboard is unreachable** (GitHub OAuth, flagged account), so there is no way to see the backups, trigger a point-in-time restore, or create the second project you would restore INTO — and there is no psql, no pg_dump and, by standing decision, no new tooling. **A confident runbook full of dashboard clicks nobody can perform is precisely the class of document this project keeps catching**, so it is not written. **But reading it end to end found something better: even if the dashboard opened tomorrow, the drill still could not have been done properly, because nobody knows what "restored correctly" looks like.** You press restore, the dashboard says success, and you have no way to tell whether you got everything, half of it, or last week's copy. **The comparison IS the drill. The button is not.** So `Vida → Engine → Backup manifest` now snapshots every table in `public` with its **exact row count**, and two choices in it are the whole point: a real **`COUNT(*)` rather than `reltuples`** — the planner's estimate is only as fresh as the last ANALYZE and is wrong by thousands on a **just bulk-loaded** table, which is exactly the state a restored database is in, so an estimate would **make a broken restore look fine**; and **counts only, never data** — a dump-everything endpoint would be one URL exfiltrating the entire customer database including `client_inboxes`, which **#554b found with no RLS at all** three hours earlier. The comparison is **exact, with no tolerance**: a restore landing 99% of `credit_transactions` has lost somebody's money and a percentage would hide it. Differences sort **money and people first** (`opt_out_blocklist` is in that list — losing it means emailing people who told us to stop). And step 6 of the runbook names the **most likely silent failure**: `smtp_pass_enc` restores perfectly as ciphertext whether or not the live `INBOX_SECRET_KEY` still decrypts it, so a restore can look complete and leave mailbox rows that exist and cannot send. **What is NOT proven is listed as its own section** — that backups exist, that a restore completes, how long recovery takes, that restored data is usable — because *"we rely on Supabase's automated backups, which we have never seen, verified, or restored from"* is the honest position and the exposure #298 was raised to name. **17 tests.** 897 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🧹 #329 — THE SEED WIPE THAT MOSTLY SHOULD NOT HAPPEN):** plan + read-only report built; **execution stays founder-only**, per the clause *"executed only on my go"*. **The honest headline is that most of the item is already done, and not by deleting anything.** #329 was logged **7 Jul** and its stated purpose is *"so Finance/Sales/GTM read the real first client cleanly"* — but **#543 (demo exclusion)** and **#453 (demo mode)** were built afterwards, and the money surfaces already strip demo + house through `getClientExclusions()` (`internal-briefs`, `money-path`, `internal`). Real payments are counted from `PURCHASE_TX_TYPES` and **not** `PAID_TX_TYPES` — a founder `manual_grant` must not make a test account permanently undeletable, which is the wrong kind of safe. **The arming gate expires:** `SEED_WIPE_ARMED` must equal **today's UTC date** AND the phrase must be typed exactly, so a variable left set in Railway cannot become a loaded gun a later deploy pulls — `FOUNDER_FLIP=1` is fine for a reversible dot, this is not reversible. The runbook's step 0 is *do nothing yet* (onboard the client first; the wipe is cleanup, never a prerequisite), and it refuses to proceed without **#298**'s backup drill, because *an untested restore is not a rollback plan*. Most likely outcome when the founder presses it: **clean**, and #329 closes without a single row deleted. **30 tests, every one about what must SURVIVE** — a wipe script's tests are not about deletion. 880 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🧯 #554c — MY MIGRATION FAILED IN PRODUCTION, AND THE SCREEN REPORTED ELEVEN SUCCESSES AS "FAILED"):** the founder ran the migrations and got `relation "public.lead_enrichment" does not exist`. **My bug: `DROP POLICY IF EXISTS <policy> ON <table>` guards the POLICY, not the TABLE** — the table reference is resolved first, so a missing table is a hard error, and node-postgres sends a multi-statement query as **one implicit transaction**, so that first line rolled back all eighteen statements. It also answered the audit's open question: **most of those tables were never created in production at all** — they come from `packages/db/src/migrations/`, a directory nothing has ever confirmed was run there, which is exactly why the live audit found none of them exposed. Same class as #565 and the leads banner — a real outcome collapsed into the scariest available word. It now reads *"11 of 12 applied … FAILED: … The rest DID apply."* **And a third, in my own test:** the guard for this migration pinned the exact literal `DROP POLICY IF EXISTS "Service role bypass" ON public.lead_enrichment`, so **fixing the production bug broke the test written to protect it** — a guard must assert the INTENT or it just freezes the defect. Rewritten to assert the tables are covered, plus two new tests that the DROP is guarded on table existence at all. Caught by the gate, not by review. 852 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🚨 #554b — THE LIVE AUDIT FOUND THREE TABLES, AND NONE OF THE FIVE I PREDICTED. `client_inboxes` HAD NO RLS AT ALL):** the founder ran the new checker against production at 11:12 and it returned **`client_inboxes` (RLS OFF)**, **`app_migrations_applied` (RLS OFF)** and **`agreement_templates`** — while every one of the five tables the repo-based verdict named came back clean. **The file verdict was wrong in BOTH directions**: it named five tables that are fine and **missed the most sensitive table in the product.** That is the entire justification for reading `pg_catalog` instead of files, delivered within an hour of building it. **`client_inboxes` holds `smtp_pass_enc`, `smtp_host`, `smtp_user` and `email` — how we log in to send as each client — and RLS was never enabled on it ANYWHERE in the repo**: `20260725_client_inboxes` created the table and simply never included the line, so there was **nothing for a file-based audit to read**. Anyone holding the public anon key could read every client's mailbox configuration. The passwords are AES-256-GCM ciphertext (`lib/inbox-secret.ts`) so this was never plaintext credentials, but hosts, usernames and addresses are plain and the ciphertext should not have been fetchable either. `app_migrations_applied` was created as a **side effect** of `20260724_one_wallet.sql` via `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, so it never passed a review that would have asked. And **`agreement_templates_admin_write` exists ONLY IN PRODUCTION** — the repo has never contained it, someone created it by hand — which is **#558 pointing the other way: production carries policies no file can show you**, so every security review this project has ever done was blind to them. Re-derived from client components only it is **five** tables. Counting server routes is the dangerous direction — it makes a table look like it NEEDS a browser-facing policy when it does not, which is exactly how a `USING (true)` survives review. 850 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🔐 #554 — RLS READ END TO END: #350 FIXED ONE LEAK AND LEFT THE CLASS; THIS FOUND FIVE MORE):** read **151 policies across 39 SQL files**. The inventory row said *"seven migration files touch row-level security"* — the surface is **five times** what the row claimed, which was the first finding. **Two Postgres rules produced every other one.** ① **A policy with no `TO` clause applies to PUBLIC** — every role, including the `anon` role the public Supabase key authenticates as, which ships in the browser bundle. So `CREATE POLICY "Service role bypass" … FOR ALL USING (true)` restricts **nothing** to the service role: **the name is the trap**, because reviewers read names and not missing clauses. ② **Permissive policies combine with OR**, so one `USING (true)` makes every careful `client_id = current_client_id()` policy on that table irrelevant — **the weakest policy on a table IS the policy.** #350 found exactly one instance (`visitor_sessions.admin_read_visits`, visitor IPs + enrichment, **confirmed in prod**), dropped it, and nobody swept the pattern. **The sweep found five more, every one created by a migration in the PRODUCTION path and dropped NOWHERE in that path** — the only DROPs sit in `supabase/staging-schema.sql`, a *staging* snapshot: **`lead_enrichment`** (enrichment on real people — the closest thing to `visitor_sessions`), **`partner_commissions`** (what each partner is owed), `partner_referrals`, `partners`, `figsy_calls`. Closed by `20260727_rls_close_public_policies`, which drops **policy definitions only** — not a row, not a column (NOTHING GETS DELETED) — and is safe because dropping a permissive policy can only ever *remove* access, the API uses the service-role key which bypasses RLS entirely, and **no browser code reads any of those tables**, verified by reading every `.from('…')` in the portal, website and admin. **The verdict is also LIVE, and that is the point of the item:** this repo has **three migration directories and two whole-schema snapshots that disagree with each other**, and #558 is the standing finding that none describes production — so a verdict read off a file is a verdict about a file. `Vida → Engine → Run RLS audit` reads `pg_policies` and `pg_class.relrowsecurity` straight out of the live database and applies the same classification; where the document and the endpoint disagree, **the endpoint wins**. A failed audit renders as an **error, never an empty pass** — "nothing found" because the query died is the worst lie available on a security screen. Note `pg_class` is queried alongside `pg_policies` deliberately: **a table with RLS switched OFF is the most dangerous state there is and has no `pg_policies` row to betray it.** **32 tests.** Two of my own scripts were wrong mid-audit and caught by checking rather than reporting: the first policy regex used `[^"\s]+` and silently dropped every multi-word policy name, mislabelling tables as "no policies"; and my own explanatory comment containing `ON t FOR ALL USING (true)` invented a phantom table `t` in the scan. 844 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (💳✂️ #352 — A CARD CHARGE ANY PROSPECT COULD TRIGGER BY REPLYING TO AN EMAIL, REMOVED):** founder-confirmed *"I confirm: yes, remove"*. Reading the block end to end found **four faults stacked in one `try`**: ① it charged in **ZAR at a rate we invented** — `Math.round(amountUsd * 19 * 100)`, a hardcoded USD→ZAR 19 inside a live card charge, so a $20 bundle billed **R380** whatever the rate actually was; ② **check-then-act**, which is the AR-14 headline — the "cooldown" COUNTED recent top-up rows and *then* charged, so two hot replies arriving together both counted zero and both charged (the real double charge `20260702_webhook_idempotency` describes; svix idempotency only ever stopped IDENTICAL replays); ③ it **could never succeed anyway** — the gate required `auto_topup_paystack_auth` and #325 pulled Paystack from the billing UI, which the code itself admitted in a comment reading *"Landmine: unreachable until a Paystack auth exists"*; ④ **nothing received the result** — `index.ts` mounted raw-body parsing for `/webhooks/paystack` and **no route was ever registered behind it**. A payment integration that cannot complete, in a currency we do not bill, at a rate we made up, with a race condition. **Removing it settles AR-14 outright instead of hardening it — there is no charge left to race** — and, the part worth remembering long after Paystack is forgotten, it takes a **payment out of a webhook any prospect can trigger by replying to an email**. **What was deliberately NOT removed**, because the founder authorised removing the CHARGE PATH and not the history (NOTHING GETS DELETED, 26 Jul): the client's stored `auto_topup_*` preferences, the columns behind them, and every historical Paystack reference in `credit_transactions` — deleting billing history to tidy a code path would be indefensible. Milla's billing page has said *"coming soon"* since #325, so no client is promised anything that has now vanished. **Three more places still named Paystack as live**, all corrected: the founder brief reported Stripe's fees as **`paystack_fee_estimate_usd`** (the arithmetic was right, the NAME was a lie on a money report — now Stripe's real 2.9% + $0.30, the fixed leg no longer quietly dropped); the Vida health page monitored `status.paystack.com`, a processor we do not use; and the launch smoke test told an operator to verify *"Paystack opens (live key)"* — a checkout that no longer exists, which they would have reported as broken. **13 regression guards, 10 proven red** — the assertion is about what is no longer in the tree, so they read the real files, and three of them assert what must **survive**. One of my own guards fired on the legitimate `api.resend.com` reply-body fetch and was narrowed to money movement rather than a hostname: **a guard that fires on the wrong thing is worse than none.** 812 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (⌛ #342 — THE LAPSE CRON HAS 500'd EVERY DAY SINCE IT WAS WRITTEN, so nothing has ever lapsed):** `/subscriptions/check-lapsed` does `.update({ status: 'lapsed' })` and **`lapsed` is not in the production `subscription_status` enum** — Postgres rejects it, the handler rethrows, the route 500s, daily. **No subscription has ever been marked lapsed, so an unpaid client keeps access forever.** Third in the family after #190 (`paused`) and #340: a green unit test proves the code does what it says, and the database says no. **But adding the value was the easy half.** Make that write succeed and it starts locking accounts on the strength of `current_period_end` — a column **only our own webhook handler writes**. One missed `customer.subscription.updated` (a deploy mid-delivery, a 500, a retry we 200'd) and the column goes stale while the client pays perfectly well: the blind bulk update would have **locked out a paying customer using our own bookkeeping error as the evidence.** So a Stripe-managed subscription is now **never** lapsed here — Stripe reports `past_due`/`unpaid`/`canceled` itself and since #340 we store those faithfully — and one drifting >3 days past its period end raises a **missed-webhook alert** instead, which is a statement about our plumbing rather than an accusation against the client. What genuinely needed this cron is the other kind: **hand-granted subscriptions from Vida**, which nothing else in the system will ever expire. `'lapsed'` also added to the reactivation list, or a client who pays after lapsing stays locked out forever — the same bug pointed the other way. **Two more found reading the handler end to end:** the whole thing returned early on `!lifecycleEmailsEnabled()`, exactly like the six other lifecycle crons — but those only send mail and **this one changes billing state, so turning off marketing email silently switched off billing enforcement**; the email is gated now, at the point the email is sent, and the lapse always runs. And the daily 500 was only ever a `console.error` plus a dead-letter row — **a billing-enforcement job that has silently stopped working is precisely what must not wait to be noticed**, so it alerts. Also put `icps.pdl_scroll_token` and `cron_claims` on the System screen's REQUIRED_SCHEMA: neither #366's nor #343's migration could be *proven* applied, and that list is only ever as good as what is on it (#558's lesson, learned again when `copilot_mode` was missing while twelve rows showed green). **22 tests, 3 proven red** by reinstating the blind behaviour — including *"THE BIG ONE: a Stripe-managed subscription is left alone"*. 799 across the suite (785 API + 14 portal); gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (💳 #340 — A DECLINED CARD BOUGHT A WORKING PRODUCT):** one ternary in `routes/stripe.ts` — `sub.status === 'active' || sub.status === 'trialing' ? sub.status : 'active'` — and the false branch is the whole defect: **everything not already good was written as `active`.** Stripe's `incomplete` (card declined at signup, subscription never started), `past_due`, `unpaid`, `canceled`, `incomplete_expired` — all stored as a live paid subscription. Every consumer gates on `.eq('status','active')` or `.in(['active','trialing'])`, so **that line was the entire authorisation decision for the paid product, and it always said yes.** **It also undid the dunning:** `invoice.payment_failed` correctly writes `past_due` and alerts, and Stripe's paired `customer.subscription.updated` carried `past_due` straight back through the ternary and out as `active` — the one path built to catch non-payment, overwritten by the bug within the same second. **The fix had to avoid the trap that is literally the next item.** Production's `subscriptions.status` is a **Postgres ENUM**, not the text+CHECK the repo's `schema.sql` claims — #190 found this the hard way (*"invalid input value for enum subscription_status: paused"*) and **#342 is the same bug still live** for `lapsed`. So "map Stripe faithfully" is only half an instruction: writing `incomplete` into an enum that has never held it fails identically, and **a failed UPDATE leaves the row on its previous value — for an existing subscription, still `active`.** The honest fix would have recreated the bug it was fixing. Hence both halves: the migration adds the values, **and every mapping carries a `fallback` drawn only from values provably already in the enum that also deny access**, so the account stays locked in the window before the migration runs and the alert names the migration. Also: `canceled`→`cancelled` (Stripe one L, our enum two — a pass-through would have been #342 again); an **unrecognised** Stripe status **denies and alerts**, because the two ways to be wrong are not equal — a denied payer is visible in minutes, free access to an unmapped state is invisible until someone reads the revenue report; and renewal re-activation widened to `unpaid`/`incomplete`/`incomplete_expired`, which only became reachable once the real states started being stored, with `paused` deliberately excluded because a pause is the client's decision, not a payment failure. **30 tests, 5 proven red** by restoring the coercion for `incomplete` alone — including *"THE HEADLINE: a card declined at signup is not an active subscription"*. 777 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (⏱ #343 — TWO REPLICAS DOUBLED EVERY EMAIL AND EVERY CHARGE, and the env var the item asked for could not have stopped it):** `startCrons()` ran on every API process with no lock and no gate; the System screen had carried the warning for weeks — *"if replicas >1, every cron in cron.ts double-fires"*. **Built both halves, because reading the deployment made the obvious fix insufficient:** Railway sets variables **per SERVICE** and every replica inherits them, so `RUN_CRONS=true` on `@kind/api` reads `true` on *both* replicas and both fire. There is no per-replica override — a process can read its own `RAILWAY_REPLICA_ID` but never how many replicas exist, which is exactly why that System row is NOT-MEASURED. So `RUN_CRONS` is a **kill switch** and a way to keep crons off other services, and the guarantee lives where both processes can see it: **`cron_claims`, primary key (job, slot)**. The INSERT *is* the lock — first process wins, every other takes a unique violation and stands down; no advisory lock to leak on a dropped connection, no leader election, just a constraint Postgres already enforces perfectly. Same shape as the `(enrollment_id, step)` backstop from #354. **`slot` is ROUNDED to the nearest minute, not truncated** — two replicas run off two clocks, and truncation puts 07:59:59.8 and 08:00:00.2 into different slots so both claims succeed and the job runs twice: *the bug, reintroduced by a rounding choice*. **Unset means ON**, deliberately — failing closed would stop every send, digest, drip and charge in the business the moment a migration lagged a deploy, and silent total automation death is the worse failure; a missing claims table therefore **runs the job anyway and alerts loudly**, which is the entire safety net. **Two things found while reading `cron.ts` end to end:** the startup line reported **"25 jobs scheduled" while 33 were** — a number nobody recounts after adding a job, printed with total confidence, now counted from node-cron's own registry (verified at runtime, not assumed); and `checkSendsStalled` does not go through `callInternal`, so without its own claim two replicas would mail the founder the same stall every hour. The System screen's replica row was rewritten rather than left to become a stale claim on the one screen whose purpose is not making stale claims. **35 tests; proven red twice** — truncating instead of rounding (3 red, including *"replicas whose clocks straddle the minute boundary still collide"*) and treating a unique violation as anything but success (3 red, including *"THE SECOND STANDS DOWN — this is the whole item"*). 733 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (📄 #366 — A CLIENT'S SECOND MONTH COULD NEVER FIND A NEW PERSON: the repeat-business killer, fixed):** `pdlSearchPeople(icp, _page = 1, size)` accepted a page number and **ignored it** — the underscore was the entire defect. Every run asked PDL for the **same first page**; run two deduped every returned person against leads the client already held and inserted **nothing**, and the client was shown *"No leads matched this ICP"* — a sentence about their targeting that was actually a sentence about our paging. The cashflow (#556) says the pack is **−$52 in month one** and *"the repeat is the business"*; this was the code that made a repeat structurally impossible. **Fixed with `scroll_token`** (PDL v5's `from` is deprecated and now 400s), persisted per ICP so it **survives between runs** — a cursor that dies with the process pages nothing. **The trap that came with it:** a scroll token belongs to ONE query, so an edited ICP would replay a cursor into an audience the client no longer targets — *a working search delivering irrelevant leads*, worse than zero. The token is therefore stored with a **fingerprint of the query**, normalised so a dragged list or a capitalisation is not a change but a new country is. **The honesty half:** a finished audience and a broken run both arrive as `inserted: 0`, and told the wrong one a client either widens an ICP that was working perfectly or abandons one that simply ran to its end — **opposite instructions**. A new `audience_exhausted` outcome now says *"we have reached the end of this audience… to find more, widen the ICP"*, the founder is alerted **on the run that discovers it** (not every run thereafter), and the ICP stops spending grants re-asking a question already answered. Widening the ICP clears the exhaustion automatically — otherwise the silent zero returns through the other door. **Two defects found while wiring, both invisible to grep:** the portal's banner was an **allowlist of two statuses written into the JSX**, so the new outcome recorded a careful sentence in the database and rendered **absolutely nothing** — the silent zero one layer above its own fix; inverted in `@kind/shared` so every non-served status shows, **including ones nobody has written yet**. And `recordRunOutcome`'s `try/catch` caught nothing that actually happens — **supabase-js returns a constraint rejection rather than throwing**, so writing a fifth status into a four-value CHECK would have lost the outcome silently and left the portal showing the previous run's message: **#342's failure mode, fixed before it bit** by widening the constraint in the same migration and alerting when the DB refuses. 39 tests, the 7 wiring ones **proven red first**. 698 across the suite; gate 6/6.
- **27 Jul (🔎 PROMPT 5 — THE FULL AUDIT, THEN ALL SIX FINDINGS FIXED):** the audit walked signup → pay → source → approve → send → reply → book and stated coverage honestly at **3.1% of the core (1,590 of 51,470 lines)**, naming what it did not read: `routes/stripe.ts` (850), `routes/icps.ts` (1,159), `lib/figsy.ts` (~2,000), `routes/calendar.ts` (588). **Six findings, every one invisible to grep and found by reading a file end to end.** ① a client pays $99 and **their checklist never ticks** — the route counted `'purchase'` while Stripe writes `wallet_topup`; ② a failed count rendered as **0**, so *"we could not check"* read as *"you have not done it"* — #565's shape on the CLIENT's side; ③ `PORTAL_URL ?? 'http://localhost:3000'` put a **dead booking link in every cold email** (latent — the founder confirmed the var IS set); ④ a sourcing failure after payment returned `{started:true, sourced:0}` — **identical to already-stocked**, console-only; ⑤ **`mailer.ts` was the transport and ignored `normalisePort`**, the guard written for it, whose own comment warns a junk port is *"a connection attempt to nowhere"* — so a bad port hung every send for the full 20s timeout; ⑥ a database read failure was labelled *"No sending mailbox assigned"*, sending an operator to configure a mailbox that was already there. **All six fixed, 29 tests, every one proven RED first** — including `expected 'http://localhost:3000/book/…' to be null`, the exact string a prospect would have received. **Two of my own guards caught my own comments** quoting the old code; both strip comment lines now, because a check that forbids you describing what you fixed is a bad check. 659 across the suite. Minted #598.

That grep now returns **`approve-lead.ts:318`**. `lib/instantly-push.ts` sits at **step 9**, after the enrol, because a lead must be paid for, revealed and in a sequence before anyone sends on its behalf; it is wrapped so it **cannot fail an approve the client has already been charged for**, and re-checks every gate itself rather than trusting the caller. **The house client is `HOUSE_CLIENT_ID`, an env var — not a name match**, because exact-string matching on a company name has bitten us twice (`MBF Holdings` vs the live `MBF Demo`, #584/#582): a name is a label a human edits, an id is not. **Unset means nothing is ever pushed** — fail closed, since guessing wrong means emailing real people from the wrong account. **Four refusals are deliberately silent** (demo · kill-switch · a client, who goes via Smartlead · no key) because alerting on the system working correctly would page the founder on **every approval** and train them to ignore it. **🛑 BLOCKED, not broken:** the founder created the key with the right four scopes and `GET /api/v2/accounts` returned **HTTP 402 Payment Required** — the plan/billing limit written into `NOT_POSSIBLE[]` on 26 Jul, now **confirmed rather than predicted**. - **27 Jul (🎨 P3-1 — EVERY ERROR IN THE VIDA COCKPIT RENDERED AS A SUCCESS: fifteen, not one):** the founder flagged the Run/Pause refusal; reading every setter and every render site under P10 showed it was **systemic**. - **27 Jul (📭 P2-2 — THE ALERT BLAMED THE PROSPECT WHEN THE FAILURE WAS OURS):** found by reading `figsy.ts` 198–240 end to end under P10. - **27 Jul (🔁 P2-1 — THE AI CLASSIFIER WAS INSIDE THE PER-CLIENT LOOP, and I put it there fixing R1):** found by reading `figsy.ts` 127–505 **end to end** under the new P10, after the founder's diagnosis — *"you grep everything off last action and never read end to end"*. - **26 Jul (📬 PROMPT 2 — THE REPLY CHAIN: five defects fixed once, in the spine every provider will flow through):** founder-authorised, *"build them provider-agnostic"*. Resend delivers replies today; Instantly and Smartlead deliver them by API next under #577 — so the matching and opt-out logic moved into `lib/reply-ingest.ts` instead of staying inside the Resend webhook, where all three providers would have grown their own copy of the same five bugs. **R1, the worst: a reply matching leads at TWO clients was dropped forever.** `.eq('email', x).maybeSingle()` **errors** on more than one row, so `lead` came back null and the handler returned a silent 200 — and two clients prospecting the same person is ordinary, because the pool is shared. Both now alert **naming the provider message id**, so the reply can be opened in the provider and answered by hand. Fixed, with a test pinning that the lookup and active-sending sets never collapse into one another. **R7:** the three opt-out writes were unchecked, and the blocklist is the single suppression source the send path reads — a silent failure means **we keep emailing someone who told us to stop**, the one failure here with a legal edge. All three checked, every failure collected, the alert **names the address**, and the blocklist is written first because the rest is bookkeeping. **21 tests, every defect proven RED against the old behaviour first.** 544 across the suite. Minted #589. Gates: api tsc · full vitest · both consoles build · doc-lint — green.

New guard test pins every MBF classification to the union of the live constraint and what the unibox renders — **proven red on `'objection'`, green when restored**. **The principle, stated once:** a demo that writes values the real path never writes is not a demo of this product — it is a second, divergent implementation that happens to look similar, and it will keep breaking against constraints the real path satisfies. So CORE-MAP.md's *"re-run it any time; it is deterministic"* was **false as shipped**, and the manifest was stale by **exactly the five files the measurement system added** — the map was **fencing out the instruments it shipped beside**, labelling them not-in-play. Then four more spec clauses that were never built: **`days since last approval`** (now via the shipped `coldState`, so it inherits #538's thresholds and the guard that an unparseable date must not suspend a payer), **send window** (read through `withinSendWindow` — the same function the send path uses, so the row cannot disagree with reality), **daily send caps**, and **replica count** — answered honestly as NOT-MEASURED, because a process can read its own replica id and never how many exist. **PDL was showing the cap with no spend against it**, which tells you nothing about whether you are near it; now `$spent of $cap (n%)`, BROKEN at the ceiling. **And the exact-string bug a third time, sitting directly below its own fix:** the stray-demo check filtered `!== 'MBF Holdings'` while the live account is *"MBF Demo"* — **so the one demo we are told to keep was being reported as a stray to delete.** Minted #582. - **26 Jul (🔁 PROMPT 1 FINISHED AGAINST ITS OWN SPEC — the missing endpoint probe, three wrong answers, and a FOURTH check that was passing without running):** founder: *"is everything in that prompt right… because you claim stuff but it is never done"*, then *"d3 gap and d4 wrong. no i want everything in step 1 done right."* So I re-read the authorising prompt line by line instead of from memory. **The gap:** it asked for *"every `/operator` endpoint answering vs erroring"* and the System screen never called one — it probed dependencies, schema, clients and Vida state and skipped the routes every Vida screen is actually made of. `operatorEndpoints()` now calls **all 20 read-only operator GETs** over real HTTP against the running process; **5xx or no answer = BROKEN**, because a route that errors renders a Vida screen empty and *empty looks identical to "nothing to do"*. **The three wrong answers**, all from the first LIVE run and all the same shape — judgement inlined in a query where no test could reach it: the **demo** was reported CRITICAL *"paid but cannot send"* when a demo cannot send **by design**; the **double-grant** check matched a wallet balance alone, flagging a client credited by `manual_grant` who never paid us; and the **MBF demo** was matched on the exact string `MBF Holdings` while the live account is `MBF Demo`, so it reported *"does not exist"* about an account sitting in the client list — right conclusion, false reason, which is not a report. Proven four ways — broke the table (`doc=999 real=92`), broke a header on a synthetic copy (`doc=777 real=30`), re-introduced the exact regression (**exit 2, not 0**), and ran doc-lint against it. **Four for four this week** (#578 ×2, #579, #581): every one a check that looked green while doing nothing — and this one was **introduced by a fix, on the same day, and shipped past a green gate**, which is the argument for a structural guard over another careful edit. Minted #580, #581. Gates: api tsc · full vitest · both consoles build · doc-lint — all green.
- **26 Jul (📮 THE SHARED `FROM` IS GONE — option B locked, and the product can finally speak to a mailbox that isn't ours):** founder pushed back on my recommendation and was right to. I had said *"A, because deliverability is reputation and theirs is warm"* — **which is not an argument for A at all**: warming is a property of the MAILBOX, not of who presses send. Provider warms it either way. Once that fell away the real trade-off was visible, and it is the founder's own point: **we have already built the sending brain** — sequence engine, send window, daily caps, A/B subjects, kill-switch, reply classifier — and **A puts a second brain in charge of *when* an email goes out**, which you cannot have. **B locked.** B also earns what A never could: a client who says *"use my own Google Workspace"* is a client A has to turn away. Founder then sent the Zapmail/SmartSenders screen as proof — **$45 = domain $18/yr + 3 mailboxes at $9/mo, on real Google and Outlook**, not a black-box sender, which is what makes B buildable at all. **Built:** `lib/sending-inbox.ts` — the sender is resolved per client from `client_inboxes`, as a **pure decision function** (18 tests) so the ordering rules and every refusal path are provable without a database. Two rules it pins, both money: a **`warming` branded mailbox never sends** (sending on it is exactly what un-warms it), so during the SOP's pooled→branded overlap the **pooled** one sends; and a half-configured preferred mailbox is **skipped, not fatal**, so a blank branded row can't take a paying client off the air. `lib/mailer.ts` — nodemailer, one transport per send (a pooled connection shared across clients could put one client's mail down another's authenticated session), returning the **same verdict shape as `interpretSend`** so every #338 rollback path — delete the phantom row, roll the claim back, alert, leave it due — keeps working byte-for-byte. `lib/inbox-secret.ts` — AES-256-GCM, fresh IV per encryption, authenticated so a tampered value **throws** rather than handing garbage to a mail server and locking out a warmed mailbox; `describeCipher` is the only thing any surface may show (25 tests). **`figsy.ts:26` — the line this whole blocker was named after — is deleted**, and `COLD_FROM` is no longer imported by the cold path, so there is no fallback left to fall back TO. **Retired the #311 `RESEND_API_KEY` guard on both cold paths**: cold mail no longer touches Resend, so gating it on that key would have blocked every send on a credential the path stopped using — the concern behind #311 now sits one level lower, in a resolve that refuses loudly instead of writing a phantom row. **🧍 Owed: `INBOX_SECRET_KEY` (`openssl rand -hex 32`) and one mailbox's SMTP details.** ⚠️ Flagged: Google and Microsoft both block plain SMTP by default (App Password / SMTP AUTH per mailbox). Gates: api tsc clean · **466** vitest · doc-lint green. Migration `20260726_inbox_smtp` (additive, idempotent) is in the Engine card.

It also rolled *"FIGSY sourcing · ICP builder · campaigns · sequences · inbox"* into one ✅ row, which reads as "sending works". Retired to `docs/archive/BUILD-STATUS-26JUL.md` with a banner naming what was wrong, not silently deleted (#555). **LAUNCH-PAD is now what he asked for:** one table per block — item · description · owner · mirrored dot — ordered **A→E**, no dates, opening with an HONEST STATE paragraph that says we cannot sell delivery today and names the line of code that is the reason (`figsy.ts:26 const FROM = COLD_FROM`). That table now exists, so the banner and its button vanished **and took every later migration with it**: two were owed (`wallet_tx_types`, `client_contact_name`) and the product offered no way to run either. This is exactly the class I had claimed to sweep for hours earlier (*"features with no UI"*) — found one and then shipped one. **Fixed:** a permanent Database card on Engine that always renders, lists every committed migration by key and title, and runs them (all idempotent, so pressing it with nothing outstanding is a no-op). `/operator/engine` now always returns the migration list rather than a single boolean. A detector that is wrong 23% of the time is how you end up believing a broken thing is fine — the same trap as eighteen unit tests passing on a bypassable gate. The gate now RESOLVES the ids against the database — this client's, surfaced, undecided — and the route works only on what the gate validated. New `lib/page-rows.ts` pages with a stable key and says out loud when it hits a ceiling. **Caught a bug I introduced doing it:** paging sorts ascending, so "first row wins" became "OLDEST approval wins" and would have shown an active client as SUSPENDED — now compared explicitly. **⑦ My MBF seed swallowed fifteen writes** (`.then(()=>{},()=>{})`), so a half-seeded demo looked built and you'd find out mid-pitch; every step is checked and the button reports problems instead of a green tick. **⑧ "Delete" deleted nothing.** `DELETE /admin/demos/:id` only set `demo_expires_at` — my button promised "removes everything, cannot be undone" over a no-op, and on the legacy Apollo demos that left **real strangers' names** in the database. - **25 Jul (🚪 THE GATES THE MONEY MODEL NEEDS — minimum 20, cold clients suspended, and the last eight flow-v2 gaps closed):** the cashflow lab made the hole concrete — a client's sender costs us **~$40/month from the day they sign**, so at $4 a lead the first **~13 approvals** buy nothing but their own inbox, and a client who approves five is a client we run a free mail service for. Founder locked two gates. **① Minimum 20, hard.** `lib/approval-batch.ts` (18 tests) + a server-side `batchGate` on **every** door into the money path — `/approve`, `/reveal` and the new `/approve-batch` — because a disabled button is a suggestion anyone can step around with a fetch. It asks for `min(20, what they actually have)` so a client we only sent 12 people isn't given an impossible gate, and it **releases entirely at 20 approvals ever**: it exists to start the relationship, not to nag someone already working with us. Milla's desk becomes pick-then-start, with a sticky bar counting them to the number. **② Cold = 30 days, we suspend.** `lib/cold-client.ts` (12 tests) + a nightly check that pauses the campaigns of anyone who hasn't approved in 30 days, warned a week out. **Caught while wiring it:** suspending pauses campaigns, and `approveLead` **fail-closes without an active campaign** — so a suspended client would have been locked out of the only action that un-suspends them. Approving now resumes a paused campaign on their own click, no operator in the loop; only `paused` is touched, never `draft` or `completed`. Never-approved is deliberately **not** cold (that client hasn't started — a different chase), and an unparseable date can never read as idle. **Then the eight open flow-v2 items, all of them:** sign-up rebranded to Milla and routed to `/milla/welcome` where the ICP is actually born · **contact capture** (name + mobile — `phone` was hardcoded to `''`, and `contact_name` ships as a migration written OUTSIDE the shared payload so an un-migrated database can't break every signup) · a **product walkthrough** (dependency-free, skips steps whose element isn't on screen) · the **dormant** state said out loud in Milla instead of a bare `—` · the **$99 asked for at the end of the ICP conversation** rather than on a banner a new client has no reason to look at · **push reminders** on day 1/3/7 (the push table existed and nothing used it) · the **test email pinned to `hello@get-kind.com`** (it fell back to whoever was logged in, which makes spam placement unjudgeable) · **People grouped by the ICP that found them** and the same top-20 marked in both consoles · **two no-shows now tell the client** with the $4 re-run choice · and `lib/suggest-times.ts` (11 tests) closes on three concrete times in the prospect's own working day when a client has no calendar — returning **nothing** for an unknown country, because a 3am proposal is worse than none. **Also fixed from the preview audit:** the next-action card now contains **the work itself** — the sequence emails, readable, with Redraft/Edit on them — instead of a button that sent you hunting through tabs, which is what the preview promised and the first build didn't deliver. Gates: api tsc + **393** vitest (+41) · both consoles build · doc-lint. **One migration to run from Vida → Engine** (`20260726_client_contact_name`), additive and safe to re-run.
- **25 Jul (💰 THE CASHFLOW LAB IS THE MODEL — founder-locked, and the old floor was missing half the stack):** founder locked the layout: **two needles at the top, every cost line an editable box** — *"we can refine numbers but the layout I understand."* Lives in the repo at `docs/CASHFLOW-LAB.html` (it was in a scratchpad, which dies with the container). **The fixed floor was wrong everywhere it appeared.** `run-costs-and-cashflow.md` §5 carried **~$190** and left out **Smartlead (~$94 — The Engine, runs every client inbox), Instantly (~$37), Zoho Mail (~$3 — the one the register itself calls out as "the tool that went missing") and the Anthropic runtime**. Honest floor: **~$457/mo**. PDL is now modelled the way it is actually billed — a **bought tier with a $98 floor**, charged as `max(tier, names × $0.28)` rather than double-counted as both a fixed line and a per-name cost. **Two numbers came out of it that change how we sell:** ① at the founder's own **$40/client/month inbox**, a client must approve **~13 leads a month just to pay for their own inbox** — below that, every extra client makes us poorer and no amount of scale fixes it; ② the **$99 pack is −$52 in month one** once $45 setup + $40 inbox are counted, not the +$29 I had modelled at a $4.50 inbox. Month two recovers it (+$121 at 50 approvals) **only if they come back — the repeat is the business, the pack is the door.** The approvals needle is explicitly an **average across the book**, so a client on 10 and one on 100 average to 55. **⚠️ Flagged for the founder, unresolved:** *names sourced per approval* is the single biggest lever and we hold two conflicting figures — flow v2 says **2**, #415 measured **~7**; at 7 the model roughly halves. It is an input box until they call it.
- **25 Jul (💰 THE $99 PACK — 100 leads, built as a quota, and two money bugs caught wiring it):** founder walked the real economics and locked it: **$99 buys 100 approved leads**, then $4 each. Cost basis: 200 records at $0.28 = **$56** (they pass on roughly half, so 200 gives a real choice at 100 approvals) · 100 worked at ~$0.06 = $6 · Stripe $3.17 · their inbox month 1 $4.50 ≈ **$70, keeping ~$29**. *(I had to correct my own table mid-decision — I'd added the fixed costs to the 50-lead row and not the rest, so "125 leads keeps you $26" was really $14. The founder had already chosen 125 on that number; corrected before anything was built, and they moved to 100.)* Built as a **counted quota, not a wallet credit**: `lib/onboarding-pack.ts` (16 tests) derives everything from rows that already exist — a purchase row, and leads with `revealed_at` — so there's no column to keep in sync. The alternative was crediting **$499** for a $99 payment so that "$4 a lead" happened to land on 100, which makes the balance mostly invention and the revenue figure untrustworthy. **Two money bugs caught while wiring it, both mine, both would have paid the client:** ① the dead-email path reverses $4 — but a pack approval charges nothing, so a dud lead would have **credited $4 they never paid**; it now hands the pack slot back instead. ② the result returned `charged: true` unconditionally, so a free approval would have told Milla it billed them; it now reflects reality and the card reads *"Included in your 100"*. Milla's hero KPI shows **leads left** rather than a $0 wallet (which would read as broken next to "100 included"), and the go-live banner now says what the $99 actually buys. **Also locked: no time limit on paid leads.** The 72h approval TTL never *expired* anything — it was a query filter, so a surfaced lead simply stopped appearing on the client's desk with no notice to anyone. Against a 100-lead pack that would have silently eaten most of what they'd bought. Removed. Gates: api tsc + **343** vitest (+16) · portal build · doc-lint. **No new SQL.**

(Self-inflicted on the way: my replacement JSX comment was missing its closing brace and broke the portal build; caught by the build, fixed, rebuilt clean.) **③ The plaintext Postgres password is scrubbed** from `docs/archive/MASTER.md` — and it turned out to be the smoking gun for the migration failure: **the password contains an `@`**, which is exactly what `passwordOf()` was truncating at before this morning's fix. So the "password authentication failed" was never a wrong password — it was my parser cutting a correct one in half. The value remains in git history, so it should still be treated as burned and rotated when Supabase access returns (a history rewrite is the founder's call, not mine). Gates: api tsc + **310** vitest · portal + admin build · doc-lint.
- **25 Jul (🔑 IPv6 SOLVED, now it's the password — and GitHub has removed the Supabase OAuth app entirely):** the pooler fallback worked: *"Tried 19 address(es); last error: **password authentication failed for user postgres**"*. That is a completely different failure from ENETUNREACH — **the database answered**, so the IPv4 route is fixed and this is now a credentials problem. Three things came out of it. ① **Supavisor reports the user as `postgres` even when we connect as `postgres.<ref>`** — it splits the dot into user + tenant — so that name in the error is expected and not evidence of a malformed username; the error text now says so, because it reads like the bug and isn't. ② **My own message was misleading:** it reported *"Tried 19 address(es)"* when the loop had stopped after two (it breaks on a non-routing error, correctly) — it printed the number of candidates GENERATED, not attempted. Fixed, and auth failures now get their own message that says plainly *"the network route is fine, this is a password problem"*, because conflating the two is what sent us chasing IPv6 twice. ③ **`passwordOf()` was truncating at the first `@`** — a generated password containing an unencoded `@` (invalid but very common in pasted connection strings) would come back short, and a short password reads back as *"password authentication failed"*: a parsing bug wearing a credentials bug's clothes. Now greedy to the last `@`, and re-encoded with `encodeURIComponent` when rebuilding. **The unblock:** the founder then reported **GitHub has removed Supabase from their authorised applications too**, so there is now no route into the Supabase dashboard at all — the password cannot be reset there. So the Engine page grows a **"Try a different Postgres password"** box that appears ONLY after the database rejects credentials (never after a network failure — that would send them chasing the wrong problem): used for one run, never stored, never logged, never in the audit row, and still not an arbitrary-SQL hole because the only statements that can run remain the reviewed committed ones. +12 tests. Gates: api tsc + **310** vitest · admin build · doc-lint.
- **25 Jul (🔌 ENETUNREACH — the migration button was never going to work, and it wasn't the SQL):** the founder pressed Vida → Engine → Run it now and got `Failed: 20260725_client_inboxes — connect ENETUNREACH 2a05:d018:cb1:bb00:…:5432`. That address is **IPv6**. Supabase's direct database host (`db.<ref>.supabase.co`) has been **IPv6-only since Jan 2024** unless you pay for the IPv4 add-on, and **Railway containers have no IPv6 egress** — so the connection died before sending a byte. Nothing was wrong with the SQL, the password, or the endpoint: the address is simply unreachable from where we run, which means this button could never have worked and the whole "run migrations from Vida" workaround was resting on a connection that cannot exist. Supabase's own answer is the **pooler (Supavisor)**, which does publish IPv4: `postgres.<ref>@aws-0-<region>.pooler.supabase.com:5432` — port 5432 is SESSION mode, which behaves like a normal connection and runs DDL (6543 is transaction mode, wrong tool for migrations). The one thing not derivable is the **region**, and the founder can't look it up because the dashboard is behind the flagged GitHub account — so `lib/db-connection.ts` generates one candidate per Supabase region (SA/EU first) and the runner **probes for a reachable one before running any SQL** (so a migration is never half-applied across two different connections), then reports which host worked. Two safety details that matter: it **only** retries on routing errors (`ENETUNREACH`/`ENOTFOUND`/timeouts) and **stops dead on a credentials error**, because replaying a bad password against 18 regions is how an account gets locked out; and `safeHost()` means the response and the audit row carry host:port only, never the password. Vida's Engine now says *which* host it connected through plus a hint to set `DATABASE_URL` to the pooler string permanently, so the fallback stops being a fallback. +18 tests (`db-connection.test.ts`). Gates: api tsc + **298** vitest · admin build · doc-lint.

That is precisely the failure the audit rule exists to stop, and I did it in the same message where I said I had checked live. Both rows corrected in the inventory rather than left to mislead the next session. **The genuine gaps, now shipped —** #317: ① **`charge.dispute.closed` was unhandled**, so a dispute WE WON left the client permanently short (we keep the cash Stripe returns AND their wallet stays clawed back — us taking money for nothing) → restores on `won` only, idempotent on `refund_restore_<id>`, and note **`type:'adjustment'` is NOT in `credit_transactions`' CHECK constraint**: the first draft used it, which would have thrown, 500'd the webhook and had Stripe retry forever while the client was never restored (`manual_grant` is the valid type; pinned in a new `refund-policy.test.ts` so it can't come back); ② **the money stopped but the WORK didn't** — a clawed-back wallet blocks the next $4 approval, yet every enrollment already mid-sequence kept emailing prospects in a refunded client's name, burning our sending reputation for someone who had just taken their money back → active campaigns now pause on refund/dispute, reversibly, with an alert. #290: ③ **`error_events` may never have existed in prod** — its migration was written to be run by hand in the Supabase editor the founder has been locked out of since, and the handler swallows the insert failure by design, so capture may have been quietly degraded the whole time → the migration is now in `pending-migrations.ts`, runnable from **Vida → Engine → Run it now**; ④ **`uncaughtException` / `unhandledRejection` only `console.error`'d**, so the crash class — the most serious kind — was the *only* one that never alerted → both now record + alert through the same hourly throttle. - **25 Jul (🔎 PRE-MERGE AUDIT of the launch path — five defects, two of them shipping-broken):** founder: *"audit this before I merge."* Right call — the audit found five, run against the built code rather than from memory. **① Would have broken the entire cockpit:** `/operator/cockpit` selected `daily_send_limit` as a column, and **there is no such column** — the cap lives in `settings.daily_send_limit` JSONB (read by the send cron in `internal.ts` and the #511 auto-tune). - **25 Jul (🚀 THE LAUNCH PATH — the shell was live, the features were not; built end to end, no SQL):** we walked the flow instead of building, and the founder named the real problem: *"the old portal, I used to create an ICP via a conversation with FIGSY… then generate people, assign them to a campaign, be suggested a campaign, edit a sequence, send a test email and hit go. Right now in Vida I can't see this process. The shell is here but the features are not."* Root cause is the same auth wall as the cockpit: every one of those endpoints is gated by `requireAuth` (a **client** Bearer JWT), and the admin app proxies with `x-admin-key` and **no client session** — so the operator could look at a campaign and never propose, fill, preview, test or run one. Built the operator twins plus the client's half (#520–#537), all on tables that already exist, so **none of it was blocked on the Supabase lockout**. **Vida** now runs the path end to end: **ICP by conversation** (`POST /operator/icp/chat`, seeded with the client's current active ICP so it refines rather than restarts; the field form stays as the precise-edit fallback) → **multi-select the people** (`GET /operator/people` — whole pool, masked emails stay masked, shows who is already enrolled) → **assign the picked ones** (`POST /operator/campaign/:id/assign`, `prepaid` so it never re-charges) → **Vida proposes the campaign, the operator approves** (`/campaign/suggest` → `/campaign/save`; deterministic when there's no LLM key — never invents, never fails the step) → **edit it** (name · the brief every email is written from · daily cap) with an **Auto-Pilot / Co-Pilot** choice (Co-Pilot writes `approve_before_send`; new campaigns start there on purpose) → **Vida drafts the sequence** against a real top-scoring lead then de-tokenises it into a template (`/sequence/suggest`) → **preview it as the prospect reads it** (`GET /operator/sequence/:id/preview`) → **email yourself a test** (`/campaign/:id/test`, subject-prefixed `[TEST · client]` — the last gate before a real prospect) → **Run it** → **see who's in it** and where each of them is (`/campaign/:id/enrollments`). Plus the **bell** (`GET /operator/alerts`, derived live from real rows — new client whose first ICP waits on us · ICP revised under a live campaign · replies to answer) and **"Ask them for these"**, which now genuinely reaches the client: `POST /operator/ask` writes into their own Milla thread, because the conversation IS the surface and no new table was needed. **Milla's half:** the thread **persists across visits** (it started blank every time, so an ask could never be seen, let alone answered), the client **revises their ICP by conversation** and it **goes live** — founder-locked, no gate on their own change — while alerting us because anyone already enrolled was picked against the OLD profile (`POST /icps/revise`), and they **see the sequence read-only** on My campaign. The top-left brand is now a link home in both consoles. **Two honesty fixes, both of which would have read as working:** assign counted successful `autoEnrollLead` calls, but that function returns `void` and bails silently on no verified email / suppression / a CRM match / exhausted FIGSY credits / an unconfigured sender — it would have reported "12 added" when 3 were, so the count now comes from `figsy_enrollments` and names what didn't make it; and because `autoEnrollLead` enrols into the client's most recent **active** campaign (it takes no campaign id), assigning to a paused campaign did nothing quietly — it now refuses with *"'X' isn't running — hit Run it first."* The `{{token}}` contract was extracted to `lib/sequence-tokens.ts` (+12 tests) so the suggest and preview halves can't drift into a literal `{{first_name}}` reaching a prospect. Gates: api tsc + **241** vitest (+12) · portal + admin build · doc-lint. **No migration.** Deferred by the founder: refining the approval gates.

So the migration now runs from **Vida**, using only what already exists: `lib/pending-migrations.ts` holds the reviewed, committed, **idempotent** statements as a string constant (NOT read from disk — `.sql` files are not copied into `dist/` by tsc, so a file read would pass locally and fail in prod), and `POST /operator/migrations/run` (admin-key gated, audited as `run_migration`) executes them against `DATABASE_URL` via node-postgres — the Supabase JS client speaks PostgREST and cannot do DDL. **The endpoint ignores the request body entirely**, so it can never become an arbitrary-SQL hole. New API: `GET /operator/cockpit` · `GET /operator/replies/:id` · `POST /operator/replies/:id/draft` · `POST /operator/replies/:id/send` · `POST /operator/campaign/:id/status`; the manual-prospect-reply path was **extracted to `lib/manual-reply.ts`** so the client and operator routes share one implementation and the demo/opt-out/kill-switch gates can never drift apart. **Milla**: the assistant is now the **full-height centre spine** (page no longer scrolls as one block), the duplicate ICP card left home (it has its own rail page), and the missing **Pipeline** page shipped — Approved → Contacted → Replied → Booked from a new `GET /leads/pipeline`, closing the "I approved it, then what?" gap. - **24 Jul (🔎 OPUS VERIFY of the console PR — caught + fixed a money-for-nothing regression):** verify pass over the overnight Sonnet build (#1146). The nav/declutter work and the Vida escape fix hold up — links, API contract (`/figsy/campaigns` column names), the middleware rewrite (no fetch() breakage, no redirect loop, `/vida/*` correctly excluded) all check out. **But the PR shipped a real, money-losing regression.** Removing the client's self-serve FIGSY console took away the ONLY UI in the entire system that could create a campaign: `POST /figsy/campaigns` is client-JWT-only, the operator/admin side has **no** campaign-create endpoint at all, and nothing auto-creates one (the only other inserts are seed/demo + clone/apply-sequence, which need an existing campaign). Meanwhile `approveLead` charges the flat $4 and then calls `autoEnrollLead(..., {force:true})`, which **bails silently** on `if (!campaign) return` — and the call is `.catch(() => {})`. Net: a client pays $99, approves a lead, is **debited $4, gets no enrolment, no outreach, no error and no refund** — and no console could fix it. **Fixed three ways:** ① `approveLead` now fail-closes *before* the charge — no active campaign ⟹ status `no_campaign`, wallet untouched, claim released, founder alerted (`sends_stalled`); it never takes money for work it can't run (the same principle as the insufficient-funds gate). ② New operator endpoint `POST /operator/campaign/start` (admin-key gated, audited as `start_campaign`, idempotent, creates **ACTIVE** — the table default is `draft`, which would leave the client just as blocked) + a "Start campaign" button on the Vida per-client page that appears exactly when a client has no active campaign, explaining why approvals are blocked. ③ Both `/leads/:id/approve` and `/reveal` return a 409 `no_campaign`, and Milla translates the known error CODES into plain English (the portal's api helper surfaces `error`, not `message`, so a client would otherwise have literally read "no_campaign"). +2 regression tests (no charge, no reversal, claim released) — the test harness's table-agnostic mock was also made table-aware so the guard is actually exercised. Gates: api tsc + **229** vitest (+2) · portal + admin build clean · doc-lint green. No migration.

### The 2–4 Aug honest-state block, the cost floor, Stripe and BLOCKS A–E (retired 6 Aug)

## 🛑 HONEST STATE — read this before anything else *(re-swept against the code 2 Aug, #608)*

> **If you have been away and remember nothing, read only this block.** It is rewritten at the end of every working session and it is the state of record for *what is left*. Status per item lives in PRODUCT-INVENTORY; why anything was decided lives in KIND-MASTER.

**Where we actually are (re-swept 4 Aug, end of the mailbox run).** The product finds people, scores them, masks them, surfaces them, takes the client's 👍, charges for it, writes the sequence, routes replies and books the meeting. **And as of 4 Aug it finally has mailboxes: 4 Google boxes on 2 fully-authenticated domains are WARMING in Instantly — clock runs to ~Tue 25 Aug**, confirmed independently by the product's own warm-up reminder. One box (`jacques@kindoutreach.com`) is registered in the engine, encrypted, status `warming`. **Nothing sends until the 25 Aug ladder** — three separate locks (warming status refused by the picker · `AUTO_OUTREACH_ENABLED` unset · the ladder itself) and the counter reads 0 sent, ever. **The plan to send-day is the table below — work it top to bottom.**

### 🔒 THE CONSTRAINT THAT GOVERNS EVERY FUTURE PROMPT — MIGRATIONS CANNOT RUN

**Do not build anything that ends with "now press Run migrations."** The Supabase dashboard is unreachable (GitHub OAuth, flagged account — a support ticket is open and unanswered), the founder does not have the Postgres password, and `DATABASE_URL` in Railway is a placeholder with no host in it, so the runner resolves nothing. **The schema is frozen for the foreseeable future.** Anything needing a schema change must either work without one or be declared NOT-POSSIBLE up front, before the work starts. *(#607's migration is registered and pending. ⚠️ **The claim that stood here — "the code was deliberately written to work with or without it" — was FALSE, and production proved it on 4 Aug:** the live schema has NOT NULL on `subscriptions.current_period_end`, #607's honest `null` bounced with 23502, and **every new-client signup failed at the front door for two days** until the founder hit it himself, signing up to test the $299. The code now tries the honest null first and retries once with a far-future sentinel — so signup works today AND self-corrects the day the migration runs.)*

### ✅ SHIPPED SINCE THE LAST SWEEP (27 Jul → 2 Aug) — merged to main, which on this repo IS live

- **#607 — the trial is gone.** Signup wrote `status:'trialing'` with a 14-day clock and two crons acted on it daily; **one emailed real people *"Your K.I.N.D trial ends in 4 days — Subscribe now"*** about a product we do not sell. Signup now writes `paused` (dormant until the $99 lands) and **both crons are retired and their endpoints refuse 410** — verified: `auth.ts` calls `signupSubscriptionRow`, `cron.ts` schedules neither job.
- **#604/#605 — the website is restored AND locked.** All **28 pages** serve again with the pre-shrink nav (verified on disk). The site is now **founder-frozen (PRODUCT-RULES P12)**: a manifest pins **110 files** and any change fails the gate until you approve it. **The site cannot change again without you saying so.**
- **#603 — Nexus is back**, with its auto-tune promises corrected to what the code actually does.
- **#406 — the portal sweep.** Four lies off the client's screens, the worst being **an invented unsubscribe number** (`replies × 0.05`) plotted on a chart. Verified: the dead notification bell is gone.
- **#413/#410/#327 — the paper matches the product.** The signup pop-up on **9 pages** promised *"Free to start… No card required"* under a $99-first model; **0 pages say it now** (verified). Terms, sub-processor lists and the CRM claim all corrected.

- **#349 — the money-write remainder.** 11 more swallowed failures closed, including one where **a client who paid would never be credited**.

**⚠️ THE ARCHITECTURE (founder, 30 Jul, unchanged).** Of Instantly's bundle we lack exactly one thing — the **warmup network**. Its sequencer, sender and unibox duplicate what we built. So **Instantly drops to Growth (~$37), warmup only**, and **FIGSY + our send path + our unibox do the outreach**. Smartlead deferred until a client is in the works.

### 💸 THE COST FLOOR — REBUILT 3 AUG FROM ACTUAL BILLS. **~$223 was wrong. It is ~$146.**

The founder said ***"i cant afford 470/month… without income coming in this is impossible to maintain"***, and opening the real invoices found the model had been overstating the floor for weeks.

**The error, and it is the biggest single number in this repo:** the *Servers + database* line read **$138** and was tagged **verified**. Actual: **Railway $15.59 + Supabase $35 = $50.59**. **Overstated by $87/mo.** Nothing changed at the vendors — the number was simply never checked against an invoice, and the "verified" tag stopped everyone who read it afterwards from checking. ⚠️ **A confident label on an unread number is worse than no number**, and this is the same defect the 29 Jun audit rule was written for.

**Then the founder's cuts, made the same day** — note what was *not* cut:

| Line | Was | Now | |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Servers + database | $138 | **$51** | Railway $15.59 + Supabase $35, off the bills. ⚠️ Railway is **usage-based** — that is the idle rate, it climbs with traffic |
| Resend | $20 | **$0** | free tier. ⚠️ ceiling not free lunch: ~3,000/mo and **~100/day** — the daily cap bites first when clients arrive |
| Apollo | $65 | **$0** | drops to the free plan **3 Sep**. August is paid, so **2,500 credits are August's prospecting already bought** |
| Claude Code | ~$152 | **~$23** | downgraded; current plan runs to **20 Aug** |
| Google Workspace | — | **+$28** | **kept** |
| Instantly Growth | $37 | **$37** | **kept** |
| Failover | $12 | $12 → **$0** | teardown now worth doing under cost pressure |

**Product floor ~$146/mo · ~$134 once the failover dies · ~$157 all-in including Claude Code.** Down from ~$470.

**Nothing on the sending path was cut, deliberately.** Killing Google + Instantly saves ~$65 and pushes first revenue further out — which is the actual problem. **At $4/approved lead, ~$134/mo is ~34 approvals a month, or roughly ONE client.** That is the whole race: one client covers the platform, two make it a business.
